## K. Database

**CQ-106** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: Never done ON swing_positions specifically for a NOT NULL column, but the safe, already-proven-elsewhere pattern for HOW to add one is documented and exercised on sibling tables in the exact same file: `ALTER TABLE <t> ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS <col> <TYPE> NOT NULL DEFAULT <constant>`, which Postgres 11+ applies as a metadata-only change (no full table rewrite/lock) since the default is a constant. `swing_positions` itself has had only one post-creation column added, and it is nullable (no NOT NULL, no default) — so 'has this ever been done on swing_positions' is DISPROVEN as fact, while the general safe mechanism is PROVEN via sibling tables. ensureSchema() runs under a pg_advisory_lock on every process boot and every ALTER is IF-NOT-EXISTS idempotent, so concurrent ECS replicas booting mid-deploy do not race.
EVIDENCE: db.ts:886 `ALTER TABLE swing_positions ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS last_mark_at TIMESTAMPTZ;` (nullable, no NOT NULL ever added to this table). Proven safe pattern on siblings: db.ts:941 `ALTER TABLE zerodte_setup_log ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS trims_taken INT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0;`; db.ts:2235 `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS signal_kinds JSONB NOT NULL DEFAULT '[]'::jsonb;` and db.ts:2249 `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS archetype TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'UNCLASSIFIED';` (both on swing_candidate_accumulation); db.ts:1264 `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS gates_blocked_json JSONB NOT NULL DEFAULT '[]';` plus similar at 1413, 1444, 1446, 2502. Migration lock: db.ts:331-336 `pg_advisory_lock($1)`; idempotent DDL pattern e.g. db.ts:2069-2074 status CHECK constraint via DO $$ ... EXCEPTION WHEN duplicate_object. Live check to firm this up: `SELECT attname, attnotnull FROM pg_attribute WHERE attrelid='swing_positions'::regclass;` against prod RDS to confirm no out-of-band NOT NULL column exists that ensureSchema doesn't reflect.

**CQ-107** | PROVEN
A: Symptom is connection errors/timeouts, not silent queueing and not merely slow queries — and this already happened live in production, documented in the code's own comments as the incident that motivated the fix. computeSafePgPoolMaxDefault divides the shared PgBouncer backend budget by REPLICA_COUNT_MAX_FOR_POOL, which defaults to the steady-state floor (REPLICA_COUNT) unless REPLICA_COUNT_MAX is explicitly set. The code comment documents: blackout-production-web's Auto Scaling runs MinCapacity=8/MaxCapacity=12 but REPLICA_COUNT only ever reflected the floor (8); RunningTaskCount routinely spikes to 9-10 during RTH and each spike lines up with a burst of 'Connection terminated due to connection timeout' (2740 events in one 10-minute window, 2026-09-01) across every DB-touching subsystem. The mitigation added is a console.warn, not an enforced cap, and is a no-op unless REPLICA_COUNT_MAX/PGBOUNCER_RESERVED_FOR_OTHER_SERVICES are actually set as env vars.
EVIDENCE: db.ts:32-39 computeSafePgPoolMaxDefault; db.ts:41-59 REPLICA_COUNT_FOR_POOL / REPLICA_COUNT_MAX_FOR_POOL with comment 'blackout-production-web's own Application Auto Scaling target runs MinCapacity=8/MaxCapacity=12 ... Measured live: RunningTaskCount for the web service routinely spikes to 9-10 during RTH, and each such blip lines up with a burst of "Connection terminated due to connection timeout" (2740 events in one 10-minute window, 2026-09-01)'; db.ts:210-236 the two oversubscription console.warn checks (steady-state and autoscaling-ceiling), explicitly noted as 'A no-op ... until REPLICA_COUNT_MAX / PGBOUNCER_RESERVED_FOR_OTHER_SERVICES are actually set'. Live check to close this: verify REPLICA_COUNT_MAX is actually set in the ECS task definition/Secrets Manager env for blackout-production-web, and pull CloudWatch Logs /ecs/blackout-production for '[db] At this service's autoscaling ceiling' to see whether the guard fires in practice.

**CQ-108** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: No purge/retention cron exists anywhere in the codebase for flow_alerts — DISPROVEN as a practice: the table is created with raw_payload JSONB and inserted_at DEFAULT NOW(), with four maintained indexes, but repo-wide grep for DELETE FROM flow_alerts / purge / retention / prune tied to this table returns zero matches. Every UW flow alert ever ingested is retained forever with no bounding mechanism. Actual current row count / growth rate / projected 2x-traffic size is UNKNOWN from this sandbox since raw Postgres TCP is blocked here.
EVIDENCE: db.ts:338-351 CREATE TABLE flow_alerts (raw_payload JSONB, inserted_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW()); db.ts:353-381 four indexes (idx_flow_alerts_created_at, idx_flow_alerts_ticker, idx_flow_alerts_ticker_created, idx_flow_alerts_recency_premium, idx_flow_alerts_event_at_recency) all maintained on every insert; Bash grep across src/lib/db.ts and repo-wide for 'DELETE FROM flow_alerts', 'purge', 'retention', 'prune' near flow_alerts returned no results. Live check to quantify: run `SELECT pg_size_pretty(pg_total_relation_size('flow_alerts')), count(*), min(inserted_at), max(inserted_at) FROM flow_alerts;` via an authenticated app-level admin route or ECS exec session (raw TCP to RDS is blocked in this sandbox per CLAUDE.md's 'Environment realities' section), then project growth to 2x traffic.

**CQ-109** | DISPROVEN
A: The premise that this is app-only enforcement is false — the FK IS enforced at the DB level with ON DELETE CASCADE. swing_position_snapshots.position_id is declared BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES swing_positions(id) ON DELETE CASCADE, a real Postgres constraint enforced on every INSERT (a snapshot referencing a non-existent parent id is rejected by Postgres itself), and ON DELETE CASCADE means any future delete of a parent row auto-deletes its dependent snapshots, so an orphan cannot arise through normal DML. No DELETE against swing_positions exists anywhere in the app code (only UPDATEs), so the cascade path is largely theoretical in practice. The one caveat: ensureSchema uses CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS, so if a database instance's swing_position_snapshots table pre-dates this FK under an older schema, ensureSchema does not retroactively ALTER TABLE ADD CONSTRAINT it in — unlike the explicit DROP+ADD repair pattern used elsewhere in this file for other FKs.
EVIDENCE: db.ts:2180-2194 CREATE TABLE swing_position_snapshots (... position_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES swing_positions(id) ON DELETE CASCADE ...). Contrast/pattern precedent for explicit FK repair: db.ts:1109-1123 comment 'DROP+ADD used to live HERE' and 'FOREIGN KEY (open_play_id) REFERENCES spx_open_play(id) ON DELETE CASCADE;' showing this codebase DOES sometimes retrofit FKs via explicit ALTER, but no equivalent retrofit exists for swing_position_snapshots → swing_positions since it was declared with the FK at table-creation time. Live check to fully confirm: `SELECT conname, confdeltype FROM pg_constraint WHERE conrelid = 'swing_position_snapshots'::regclass AND contype='f';` against prod RDS, and `SELECT count(*) FROM swing_position_snapshots s LEFT JOIN swing_positions p ON s.position_id=p.id WHERE p.id IS NULL;` to directly count any existing orphans.

Retained for historical reference; update with RDS restore procedures before the next quarterly drill.'; docs/ops/PITR-RESTORE-DRILL.md:26-31 the RPO/RTO table: '| RPO (max data loss) | _TBD_ | | RTO (time to fork ready) | _TBD_ | | Last drill date | _TBD_ |'; grep of docs/audit/FINDINGS.md for RPO/RTO/backup/restore-drill returned only the PITR doc reference, no drill-completion record. ## L. Redis / Cache

**CQ-111** | DISPROVEN
A: No environment-based prefixing exists — DISPROVEN as a live risk today, though the underlying design pattern (single hardcoded prefix, env-selected only by which REDIS_URL you point at) would have been a real risk while staging existed. `src/lib/shared-cache.ts` hardcodes the literal string `blackout:` on every Redis key (`sharedCacheGet`: `` `blackout:${key}` `` at line 94; `sharedCacheSet` line 150; `sharedCacheSetNx` line 182; `sharedListAppend`/`sharedListRange` via the `full = \`blackout:${key}\`` var at lines 271/302) — there is no `NODE_ENV`, `STAGE`, or similar token folded into the prefix anywhere in the file (confirmed via grep: only `process.env.REDIS_URL` appears, no other env read). Isolation between environments was therefore NEVER prefix-based — it depended entirely on prod and staging pointing at physically separate ElastiCache clusters via different `REDIS_URL` secrets. Per `CLAUDE.md`'s 'Vector E2E validation' section, staging (`blackout-staging-*`, including its own ElastiCache) was fully decommissioned 2026-07-25, so today there is exactly one environment and one Redis, making the cross-environment-poisoning scenario moot in practice. If a second environment (e.g. a revived staging or a preview deploy) is ever stood up again, it would need its own distinct `REDIS_URL`, because the code gives it no other isolation — that remains a live gap in the code's design, just not an active risk while only production exists.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/shared-cache.ts:94,119,150,182,201 (`blackout:${key}` hardcoded); src/lib/shared-cache.ts:60 (`redisEnabled()` reads only REDIS_URL, no env-name token); CLAUDE.md 'Vector E2E validation — STAGING DECOMMISSIONED (2026-07-25)' confirms only one environment/Redis now exists.

**CQ-112** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: Within a single ECS task (Node process), fetchGexHeatmap coalesces to exactly ONE in-flight rebuild per ticker via a plain in-memory `Map` (`heatmapInflight`, polygon-options-gex.ts:1384) — a concurrent cache-miss caller finds the existing build promise (line 2939) and awaits it (capped by `awaitHeatmapBuildWithBlockCap`, line 2941) rather than starting a second Polygon chain fetch; forceRefresh gets its own coalescing key (`${cacheKey}:force`, line 2927) so forced and normal requests don't share one build. This is proven by code and by the existing unit test `fetchGexHeatmap caps inflight/cold builds with gexHeatmapMaxBlockMs (never-block)` (polygon-options-gex.test.ts:138) plus the adjacent assertion that 'inflight coalesce must not return the raw promise' (line 155). HOWEVER `heatmapInflight` is a bare module-level `Map`, not backed by Redis/`sharedCacheSetNx` — it has NO cross-replica coordination. So on a genuine SPX/SPY expiry during RTH with the web service scaled to N ECS tasks, each of the N replicas can independently miss its own local cache and its own local in-flight map at the same instant, producing up to N concurrent full chain rebuilds against the shared 40-RPS Polygon budget — the exact thundering-herd this question asks about is NOT guarded against across replicas, only within one process. This is architecturally identical to the gap `sharedCacheSetNx`'s own doc-comment (shared-cache.ts:156-176) explicitly calls out as the reason cron overlap guards use a Redis NX lock instead of an in-memory one — but that pattern was not applied here. LIVE CHECK THAT WOULD PROVE THE MAGNITUDE: describe `blackout-production-web`'s ECS `desiredCount` (`aws ecs describe-services --cluster blackout-production-cluster --services blackout-production-web`) to get N, then during/near an RTH TTL boundary, grep CloudWatch Logs `/ecs/blackout-production` across task IDs for concurrent `buildGexHeatmapUncached`-adjacent Polygon chain-fetch log lines for the SAME ticker within the same ~100ms window — if N task IDs each log a fetch start in that window, the herd is confirmed at exactly N concurrent rebuilds, not the single coalesced one the in-process test proves.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/providers/polygon-options-gex.ts:1384 (heatmapInflight = new Map, in-memory, no Redis); lines 2923-2954 (single-flight coalescing logic + comment 'coalesce concurrent cache-miss builds for this ticker'); src/lib/providers/polygon-options-gex.test.ts:138,155 (in-process coalescing test only); src/lib/shared-cache.ts:156-194 (sharedCacheSetNx — the Redis-backed distributed lock pattern that exists elsewhere in the codebase but is not used to gate the heatmap build itself, only to share the already-computed VALUE).

**CQ-113** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: All replicas DO subscribe, and pubsub eviction IS effectively immediate for the tier-cache layer itself — but there is a separate, undocumented staleness path (the JWT fast-path) that the pubsub mechanism does not cover, so 'max stale tier after cancel' is not simply the pubsub latency. Mechanism: `tier-cache.ts` keeps tier in a per-replica in-memory `Map` (`tierCache`, line 25) with a 60s TTL; `ensureTierCacheSubscription()` (line 77) lazily subscribes each replica, on its first `resolveUserTier` call, to the Redis pub/sub channel `blackout:tier:changed` via `redis-pubsub.ts`'s `redisSubscribe`; `publishTierChanged(userId)` (tier-cache.ts:101) evicts locally AND publishes to that channel, so every already-subscribed replica's handler (`invalidateTierCache`) fires as soon as Redis delivers the PUBLISH — no polling delay, no batching. `publishTierChanged` is wired into every membership-changing write path: the Whop webhook (`src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts:118,268,380`), the Clerk webhook, `/api/membership/sync`, `/api/admin/users/*`, and `membership.ts:351`. HOWEVER: `resolveUserTier(userId, sessionClaims)` (tier-cache.ts:126) has a fast path (lines 140-143) that trusts the Clerk session JWT's own `tier` claim (`tierFromSessionClaims`) WITHOUT ever consulting `tierCache` or being touched by pubsub, whenever the claim says 'premium' or 'community' — and BOTH the page-render gate (`auth-access.ts:24`) and the API gate (`market-api-auth.ts:45`) call `resolveUserTier` WITH `sessionClaims` passed through, so both hit this bypass. Only the long-lived SSE re-check path (`sse-stream-entitlement.ts:19`, `recheckSseUserEntitlement`) deliberately omits session claims, with the comment 'Intentionally omits session JWT claims so Whop cancellation / publishTierChanged invalidation is honored on the next tick — connect-time auth() is not enough' (lines 8-11) — the team explicitly identified this exact staleness mode for SSE and fixed it there, but the SAME bypass remains live for ordinary page loads and market-data API polls. Effective max-stale-after-cancel for those paths is bounded by whenever the user's Clerk session JWT is next reissued with a refreshed `tier` claim (Clerk's own client-side session-token refresh interval, not configured in this repo), not by the sub-second pubsub latency. LIVE CHECK: cancel a temp premium user's Whop membership mid-session (reusing `scripts/audit/lib/clerk-audit-user.mjs`'s temp-user flow) while holding an already-issued browser session, and time-stamp when a protected API route (e.g. `/api/market/gex-heatmap`) starts 403'ing versus when `publishTierChanged` fired server-side — the gap between those two timestamps is the true answer, and code evidence says it will exceed the pubsub-only latency whenever the client still presents a not-yet-refreshed JWT carrying the old tier claim.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/tier-cache.ts:25,36,77-93,101-106,126-160 (cache + subscription + publish + JWT fast path at lines 140-143); src/lib/redis-pubsub.ts:84-128 (redisPublish/redisSubscribe, real-time delivery, no batching); src/lib/clerk-session-claims.ts:15-18 (tierFromSessionClaims reads JWT claim directly); src/lib/auth-access.ts:24 and src/lib/market-api-auth.ts:45 (both pass sessionClaims into resolveUserTier, hitting the bypass); src/lib/sse-stream-entitlement.ts:8-19 (SSE path deliberately omits claims, proving the team already recognizes this exact gap for one surface but not the others).

**CQ-114** | PROVEN
A: Fail-open, explicitly and by design — and there is no alert on the fail-open path, only a console.warn. `claimWhopEvent(eventId)` (src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts:50-63) does `SET whop:event:{id} 1 EX 86400 NX`; on any Redis error it returns `true` with the code comment 'fail-open: if Redis is down, process anyway' (line 61), and when `REDIS_URL` is unset it returns `true` unconditionally with 'no Redis → can't deduplicate, always proceed' (line 52) — both branches let the webhook process rather than blocking or 403/500'ing. The consequence named directly in the question (duplicate entitlement grants) is real for anything that fires as a SIDE EFFECT of processing (duplicate ops-Discord notices via `notifyOpsDiscord`, duplicate member emails via the billing-lifecycle-email calls) but is PARTIALLY mitigated for the tier itself: `syncEmailTier`/`syncWhopMembershipForEmail` recompute tier from Whop's own current membership state each call rather than incrementing anything, so reprocessing the same event twice sets the same tier twice rather than granting it 'again' — the double-processing risk is real but its blast radius is duplicate notifications/emails more than a compounding entitlement bug. On the alerting side: I searched the route for `notifyOpsDiscord` calls (6 call sites, lines ~149,331,347,382,410,431,486) and none of them are wired to the Redis-unavailable branch — that branch only does `console.warn` (line 60) with no Discord/PagerDuty/telemetry call, so there is currently NO alert if Redis is down during a webhook burst; an operator would only learn about it by reading CloudWatch Logs for that warning string. This is a genuine gap worth flagging even though the question only asked whether one exists.
EVIDENCE: src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts:50-63 (claimWhopEvent, fail-open on both no-Redis and Redis-error branches, comments say so explicitly); src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts:60 (console.warn only, no notifyOpsDiscord); route.ts:115-119 (syncEmailTier recomputes tier from source-of-truth rather than incrementing, limiting but not eliminating duplicate-processing blast radius).

**CQ-115** | UNKNOWN
A: Cannot be answered from static code — this requires a live Redis MEMORY STATS/`--bigkeys` scan or CloudWatch ElastiCache metrics, neither of which is reachable from this sandbox (raw TCP to Redis is blocked per CLAUDE.md's 'Environment realities' section, and the maxmemory-policy is an ElastiCache parameter-group setting that lives in the separate blackout-infra repo/AWS console, not in this repo). What the code DOES establish: (1) a set of candidate high-volume key patterns — `blackout:gex-heatmap:{root}` (full strike×expiry GEX/VEX/DEX/CHARM matrices per ticker, likely the single largest per-key payload given the multi-metric-block shape in polygon-options-gex.ts), the append-only Vector rail lists written via `sharedListAppend`/RPUSH every ~5s per ticker/horizon (bounded by `maxLen` + periodic `ltrim`, so individual lists are capped, but the key COUNT scales with tickers×horizons), `blackout:desk:sticky:*` (gexWalls/strikeLevels/gammaFlip/gammaRegime/unifiedTape/spxFlowBriefs), `blackout:ratelimit:{key}:{ip}:{bucket}` (short-TTL, one per IP per rate-limited route), and the in-memory-fallback comment in shared-cache.ts (lines 5-9) naming `quote:`/`nw:optmark:`/`server:` prefixes as ones that 'accumulated for the whole process lifetime' before a bound was added — these are all real key families but I have no live SIZE data to rank them. (2) The `tier:*` premise in the question as literally stated does not hold: tier data is NEVER stored as a Redis key at all — `tier-cache.ts` keeps it in a per-replica in-memory `Map` only, and the only Redis touchpoint for tier is the transient PUBLISH on channel `blackout:tier:changed` (a fire-and-forget message, not a persisted key subject to eviction). So `gex-heatmap:*` cannot evict a `tier:*` key because no such key exists to evict — the real analogous risk would be `gex-heatmap:*` (or any other high-churn prefix) evicting `blackout:ratelimit:*` keys (which DO exist in Redis and gate abuse) or the `whop:event:*` idempotency-claim keys (CQ-114), which is a plausible but unverified risk. (3) One piece of real historical live evidence exists in-repo: `vector-wall-persist.ts:130-133` records that on 2026-08-07 CloudWatch showed ElastiCache memory at 15-20% with ZERO evictions on both nodes during an investigated data-regression window — a live data point, but 5 weeks stale and not a current 'top 5 keys' answer. LIVE CHECK THAT WOULD PROVE THIS: `aws elasticache describe-cache-clusters --show-cache-node-info` + `aws cloudwatch get-metric-statistics --namespace AWS/ElastiCache --metric-name Evictions/BytesUsedForCache/DatabaseMemoryUsagePercentage` for `blackout-production-redis-rg`, and (via an ECS exec session onto a task with redis-cli, since direct TCP is blocked here) `redis-cli --bigkeys` or `MEMORY USAGE <key>` sampled across the `gex-heatmap:*`, `ratelimit:*`, and vector-rail key families, plus `CONFIG GET maxmemory-policy` to settle whether eviction is `allkeys-lru` (any key at risk) or `volatile-lru`/`noeviction` (TTL'd keys only, or writes simply fail/error at the memory ceiling instead of evicting).
EVIDENCE: src/lib/shared-cache.ts:1-25,207-220 (key-prefix families: quote:/nw:optmark:/server:, desk:sticky:*, no per-key TTL differentiation visible for eviction priority); src/lib/ip-rate-limit.ts:164 (blackout:ratelimit:{key}:{ip}:{bucket} — real Redis-resident auth-adjacent keys); src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts:55-56 (whop:event:{id} — real Redis-resident idempotency keys, 24h TTL); src/lib/tier-cache.ts:25,36 (tier itself is in-memory only; blackout:tier:changed is a pub/sub channel, not a stored key); src/features/vector/lib/vector-wall-persist.ts:128-133 (2026-08-07 live CloudWatch data point: 15-20% memory, 0 evictions — stale, not current).

## M. APIs

**CQ-116** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: No `/api/market/*` route is versioned by header or path (no `X-Api-Version`, no `apiVersion`, no `/v1/`/`/v2/` prefix anywhere under `src/app/api/market`). The only `/v2/` or `/v1/` strings found are unrelated: Polygon's own external URL path (`/v2/aggs/ticker/...` in `nighthawk-morning-confirm/route.ts:148`) and an internal module namespace `src/lib/swing/v2/` (a code-organization label, not an API contract version). So there is no formal API-versioning scheme — DISPROVEN as literally asked. What exists instead of versioning is an additive-only discipline: the Largo product contract (`docs/audit/LARGO-PRODUCT-CONTRACT.md`, `src/lib/largo/contract/product-read.ts`) states `ProductRead<T>` must WRAP a product's own type and explicitly forbids 'flattening' as a violation; `docs/audit/FINDINGS.md` has many fixes explicitly noting 'no schema change' as a deliberate constraint (e.g. lines ~2749, ~5883, ~12714, ~13797 — one entry even declines a rename because it 'changes the shape of a persisted column... splitting any historical series'). For the highest-stakes external consumer (the native iOS app), the codebase uses a dedicated PROJECTION layer instead of versioning: `/api/mobile/signals` (`src/app/api/mobile/signals/route.ts:27-38`) explicitly exists because 'the two source payloads... evolve constantly. If iOS bound directly, every backend rename would break the app' — so it projects into a stable `Signal` type (`src/lib/mobile/signals-projection.ts`) that mirrors the iOS `SignalLifecycle` enum 'verbatim.' That is a real backward-compat mechanism (isolation via a stable projection, not versioning), but it's not written down anywhere as a formal 'policy' document, and I found no example of an actual breaking schema change to a `/api/market/*` route being merged in the last 90 days (searched FINDINGS.md for 'breaking'/'schema change' — every hit is either a deliberate non-change or an unrelated bug fix). LIVE CHECK to firm this up: `git log --since='90 days ago' -p -- 'src/app/api/market/**/route.ts' | grep -B5 'delete.*field\|rename'` to look for an actual field removal/rename in a shipped route, and diff historical `/api/mobile/signals` responses against the iOS app's `SignalLifecycle.swift` decoder to confirm the projection has in fact absorbed a backend change without an app update.
EVIDENCE: src/app/api/market (29 subdirectories) grepped for /v1/, /v2/, X-Api-Version, apiVersion — zero matches. src/lib/largo/contract/product-read.ts + docs/audit/LARGO-PRODUCT-CONTRACT.md (additive-only rule). src/app/api/mobile/signals/route.ts:27-38 (projection rationale, quoted). src/lib/mobile/signals-projection.ts:1-14 (SignalPhase mirrored from Swift enum). docs/audit/FINDINGS.md multiple 'no schema change' entries.

**CQ-117** | PROVEN
A: HELIX flows REST uses a hybrid offset/cursor scheme, not classic numeric-offset pagination: the client sends `limit` and an optional `before` timestamp cursor; the server clamps `limit` and returns `has_more`/`next_before` for the client to pass back as the next page's `before`. The cap is hard-coded and enforced server-side regardless of what the client requests: `src/app/api/market/flows/route.ts:36-39` computes `pageLimit = Math.min(Math.max(Number(sp.get('limit') ?? 500) || 500, 1), HELIX_FLOW_MAX_LIMIT)`, where `HELIX_FLOW_MAX_LIMIT = 5000` is defined in `src/features/helix/lib/helix-flow-limits.ts:7` ('Hard max rows per API request (Postgres LIMIT)'). So a client request of `limit=100000` computes `Math.min(Math.max(100000,1), 5000) = 5000` — the request is silently clamped to 5000 rows, never honored as-is, and never causes an unbounded Postgres/UW fetch. `since_hours` is separately clamped to `HELIX_FLOW_MAX_SINCE_HOURS = 720` (30 days) at lines 43-46. Both the Postgres path (`readFlowsMemberCached`, line 60-67) and the live-UW fallback path (`fetchMarketFlowAlerts({limit: pageLimit + 1, ...})`, line 92) receive the already-clamped `pageLimit`, so there is no code path where the raw client-supplied limit reaches the DB or UW layer. This closes the DoS vector the question raises: a `limit=100000` request is not a resource-exhaustion risk given this code, though there is no automated regression test in this pass confirming the clamp at runtime (only static-code proof).
EVIDENCE: src/app/api/market/flows/route.ts:36-46 (clamp logic, quoted above), lines 60-67 and 92 (clamped pageLimit passed to both DB and UW paths). src/features/helix/lib/helix-flow-limits.ts:7,13 (HELIX_FLOW_MAX_LIMIT=5000, HELIX_FLOW_MAX_SINCE_HOURS=720). Live check to fully close the loop: `curl "$BASE/api/market/flows?limit=100000" -H "Cookie: __session=$JWT"` against prod and confirm the response's `count`/`flows.length` is <=5000.

**CQ-118** | PROVEN
A: The default upstream fetch timeout for Polygon (and every other external provider) in the request path is 15 seconds, defined once and applied uniformly: `DEFAULT_FETCH_TIMEOUT_MS = 15_000` in `src/lib/api-tracked-fetch.ts:67`, with the comment explaining why: 'Bare fetch() has no default timeout — a stalled upstream TCP connection (seen live against api.massive.com during Polygon GEX-heatmap slowness) hangs the calling request indefinitely... 15s is generous versus the ~1-3s p99 for these APIs but well under Vercel/Next's own route timeout.' It's enforced via `AbortSignal.timeout(timeoutMs ?? DEFAULT_FETCH_TIMEOUT_MS)` (line 148) inside `trackedFetch()`, the single egress choke-point every provider call goes through (Polygon via `polygonTrackedFetch` in `src/lib/providers/polygon-rate-limiter.ts:25`, which `polygon-options-gex.ts` imports and uses for the GEX-heatmap chain fetch). When the timeout fires, `trackedFetch` throws, and for the request-path GEX-heatmap route the member does NOT see a raw 504 or a partial/half-built JSON payload — the route's top-level try/catch converts it to a graceful HTTP 200 with an explicit unavailable-state envelope: `src/app/api/market/gex-heatmap/route.ts:523-534` — `catch (error) { ... return NextResponse.json({ available: false, underlying: ticker, error: 'GEX heatmap build failed' }, { status: 200, ... }) }`, with the code comment explicitly stating the intent: 'Unify the "no data" contract: a build throw returns 200 {available:false} (same as a null chain above and the quote/explain routes) so the client renders its graceful empty state instead of a 502 red banner.' So the member sees a clean 200 'no data available' response, never a hung connection, never a 504, and never a partially-populated/corrupt payload — by design.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/api-tracked-fetch.ts:61-67 (DEFAULT_FETCH_TIMEOUT_MS=15000, comment), line 148 (AbortSignal.timeout wiring). src/lib/providers/polygon-rate-limiter.ts:25 (imports trackedFetch as the funnel for all Polygon REST). src/app/api/market/gex-heatmap/route.ts:523-534 (catch block returning 200 {available:false} on any build failure including a timeout).

**CQ-119** | PROVEN
A: The `api_telemetry_events` table (schema written by `src/lib/api-telemetry-persist.ts:6-42`) and its in-memory mirror (`src/lib/api-telemetry.ts`) instrument OUTBOUND PROVIDER CALLS via `trackedFetch()`, not inbound Next.js route handlers directly — so instrumentation coverage is really 'which routes' critical path calls an external provider through trackedFetch,' not literally every `/api/market/*` route. Confirmed callers of `trackedFetch` (`src/lib/api-tracked-fetch.ts`): `src/lib/providers/polygon-rate-limiter.ts`, `src/lib/providers/uw-rate-limiter.ts`, `src/lib/providers/unusual-whales.ts`, `src/lib/providers/web-search.ts`, `src/lib/providers/queue-budget.ts`, `src/lib/engine.ts`, `src/lib/admin-api-dashboard.ts` — i.e. every Polygon, Unusual Whales, web-search, and blackout_engine call is instrumented. Routes whose critical path is a pure Postgres read with no external fetch (e.g. `/api/market/flows` when `dbConfigured()` is true, reading via `readFlowsMemberCached`) are 'blind' in this system — I found no `recordApiCall({provider: "postgres", ...})` call site anywhere outside test files and one admin webhook route, even though `ApiProviderId`/`byProvider` in `api-telemetry.ts:298-313` defines a `postgres` bucket, implying it was designed to be populated but is not actually wired for DB-only routes. Separately, `src/lib/admin-route-errors.ts` records only synthetic, latency-0 failure events for admin routes (`recordApiCall({..., latency_ms: 0, synthetic: true})`), which explicitly do NOT feed the real latency distribution. For `GET /api/market/gex-heatmap` specifically: YES, p99 latency IS tracked, because its build path calls `fetchGexHeatmap` (`src/lib/providers/polygon-options-gex.ts`) which uses `polygonTrackedFetch` → `trackedFetch`, and `recordApiCall` (`src/lib/api-telemetry.ts:264-265`) computes `p95_latency_ms: percentile(latency_samples, 95)` and `p99_latency_ms: percentile(latency_samples, 99)` per templated Polygon endpoint key, bounded by `MAX_SAMPLES`/`MAX_ENDPOINT_STATS` (LRU-evicted). So the answer is nuanced: p99 is tracked for the Polygon fetch(es) *behind* gex-heatmap (keyed by the polygon endpoint template, e.g. an options-chain path), not for the gex-heatmap Next.js route's own end-to-end wall-clock latency (which also includes in-process GEX math, UW cross-validation, cache lookups) — those extra milliseconds are invisible to this system.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/api-telemetry-persist.ts:6-42 (api_telemetry_events INSERT). src/lib/api-telemetry.ts:230-266 (endpointStats keyed by templated endpoint, p95/p99 percentile computation) and :298-313 (byProvider buckets incl. 'postgres' with no live writer found). src/lib/api-tracked-fetch.ts callers grep: polygon-rate-limiter.ts, uw-rate-limiter.ts, unusual-whales.ts, web-search.ts, queue-budget.ts, engine.ts, admin-api-dashboard.ts. src/lib/providers/polygon-options-gex.ts:13 imports polygonTrackedFetch. src/lib/admin-route-errors.ts:14-24 (synthetic, latency_ms:0, admin-only). LIVE CHECK: hit the admin API dashboard (whatever route surfaces getApiTelemetrySnapshot/endpointStats — likely under /api/admin) after driving traffic to gex-heatmap and confirm a polygon-endpoint entry with populated p99_latency_ms appears; separately confirm a DB-only route like /api/market/flows produces zero api_telemetry_events rows even under load.

**CQ-120** | PROVEN
A: A member CANNOT authenticate as a cron caller by guessing/leaking CRON_SECRET from the client bundle, because CRON_SECRET is never referenced in any client-side code and never sent to the browser. `authorizeCronOrTierApi` (`src/lib/market-api-auth.ts:61-71`) is the gate in question: it first checks `isCronAuthorized(req)` (lines 7-17), which reads `process.env.CRON_SECRET` server-side and does a `timingSafeEqual` constant-time comparison against the request's `Authorization: Bearer <token>` header — this all runs inside a Next.js API route handler (server-only execution; `market-api-auth.ts` has zero `"use client"` directives, confirmed `grep -c "use client"` = 0). A member could only exploit this if the literal secret value ever reached a client-shipped artifact. I grepped every `CRON_SECRET` reference in `src/` (34 hits) and every one lives in `src/app/api/**/route.ts` (Next.js Route Handlers — server-only, never bundled to the client by the Next.js build) or in plain `src/lib/*.ts` files with no client directive (`cron-dispatch.ts`, `admin-cron-health.ts`, `rth-warm-leader.ts`). I additionally grepped the built client bundle at `.next/static` for the literal string `CRON_SECRET` and found ZERO matches, confirming the value (or even the variable name) never made it into any shipped JS chunk or source map in this checkout. There is also no `NEXT_PUBLIC_CRON_SECRET` or equivalent public-prefixed env var anywhere in the repo. The remaining leak vector the question raises — 'from an error message' — was not found either: none of the CRON_SECRET-referencing files echo the secret value into a thrown Error or a JSON response body (e.g. `cron-dispatch.ts:120` returns only the string 'CRON_SECRET not configured on this deployment', never the value). I did not find and could not run a live production HTTP probe in this pass (no ability to fetch the LIVE deployed .next bundle from here) — the code-level proof is complete but a live confirmation against the actual deployed artifact would close the loop fully.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/market-api-auth.ts:7-17 (isCronAuthorized, timingSafeEqual against process.env.CRON_SECRET), :61-71 (authorizeCronOrTierApi calling it first). grep -c 'use client' src/lib/market-api-auth.ts = 0. grep 'CRON_SECRET' across src/ = 34 hits, all in src/app/api/**/route.ts or plain lib files with no client directive (full list captured during investigation, e.g. src/lib/cron-dispatch.ts:112,120; src/lib/admin-cron-health.ts:511-522; src/app/api/cron/nighthawk-morning-confirm/route.ts:115-116). grep -rln 'CRON_SECRET' .next/static = zero matches (checked in this pass). LIVE CHECK to fully close: fetch the deployed prod bundle's JS chunks and any exposed sourcemaps (`curl https://blackouttrades.com/_next/static/... | grep -i cron_secret`) and confirm zero matches there too, plus attempt `curl -H 'Authorization: Bearer <guessed-or-leaked-value>' https://blackouttrades.com/api/market/vector/spy-volume` to confirm a wrong/absent secret still 401/403s a member session.

## N. WebSockets / SSE

**CQ-121** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: PR #3906 is real and its code matches the claim, but no timed live reproduction was run. `recheckSseUserEntitlement(userId, minTier, tool?)` (src/lib/sse-stream-entitlement.ts:13-30) calls `resolveUserTier(userId)` — Clerk-backed live lookup, NOT the connect-time session JWT claims — and returns 'forbidden' if `!tierAtLeast(tier, minTier)`. Vector's stream route calls this every tick (TICK_MS=1000ms) BEFORE sending a frame (route.ts:76-90); on 'forbidden' it emits `event: error\ndata: {"error":"Forbidden — upgrade required"}\n\n` and closes. Downgrade latency is bounded by: the 1s tick interval, and how fast `resolveUserTier` sees the new tier. It uses a 60s TTL cache (tier-cache.ts:26 `TIER_CACHE_TTL_MS=60_000`), but Whop cancellation triggers `publishTierChanged(userId)` (webhook/whop/route.ts:118/268/380) which evicts the cache immediately and fans out cross-replica via Redis pub/sub (tier-cache.ts:20-23) — so the very next 1s tick after the webhook lands should see the downgraded tier rather than waiting the full 60s. I did not have a live Whop cancellation to time.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/sse-stream-entitlement.ts:13-30 (recheck logic); src/app/api/market/vector/stream/route.ts:22,76-90,131-134 (1s tick + recheck-then-close); src/lib/tier-cache.ts:26 (60s TTL), :101 (publishTierChanged evicts+broadcasts); src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts:118,268,380 (cancellation calls publishTierChanged); git log confirms `bd2561d28 fix(entitlement): SSE recheck resolves tier without stale JWT claims (#3906)`. LIVE CHECK to prove timing: mint temp premium Clerk user (scripts/audit/lib/clerk-audit-user.mjs), open `GET /api/market/vector/stream?ticker=SPX` with its `__session` cookie, then call `POST /api/admin/users/{id}` to downgrade tier (triggers publishTierChanged) or simulate the Whop webhook, and measure wall-clock time to the `event: error` frame on the open connection.

**CQ-122** | PROVEN
A: Backpressure DROPS THE CONNECTION, never individual events, and the policy is identical across routes (no per-route difference between flows and vector). `sseBackpressureExceeded(desiredSize, maxQueuedChunks=64)` returns true once `controller.desiredSize <= -maxQueuedChunks`. Every stream route using it (vector, flows, zerodte/marks, spx/pulse, stocks/spot-stream, admin/apis) follows the same pattern: on true, call cleanup() and controller.close() — the whole SSE connection is torn down, not a single frame silently skipped. The client's `createReconnectingEventSource` then sees `onerror`, fires `onClose`, and reconnects with exponential backoff (1000ms→30000ms), so the tab silently re-establishes — nothing tells the member 'you were dropped for lagging' vs. a normal blip.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/sse-backpressure.ts:30-38 (predicate); src/app/api/market/vector/stream/route.ts:91-99 and src/app/api/market/flows/stream/route.ts:83-91 (identical close-on-exceed pattern, confirmed via grep across all 6 stream routes that import sseBackpressureExceeded); src/lib/api.ts:893-950 (createReconnectingEventSource onerror→backoff reconnect, retryMs 1000→30000 doubling).

**CQ-123** | PROVEN
A: Yes, heartbeats exist, but the mechanism and interval are INCONSISTENT across routes, and there is no explicit client-side 'missed-heartbeat' watchdog — reconnect relies solely on the browser EventSource's native onerror. Vector, zerodte/marks, spx/pulse, and stocks/spot-stream all send a true SSE COMMENT `: heartbeat\n\n` every 15,000ms (invisible to onmessage, purely keeps the connection alive through proxies/LBs). flows/stream is different: its heartbeat sends a real DATA event `data: {"type":"heartbeat","ts":...}\n\n` every 25,000ms, which DOES fire onmessage; the flows client parser only forwards `type==="flow"` payloads, so heartbeats are received but discarded, not acted on. Client-side `createReconnectingEventSource` has no independent heartbeat-timeout timer of its own — only the native EventSource onerror triggers reconnect, with 1s→30s exponential backoff. So a 'frozen' tab where the transport itself doesn't error (heartbeats still flowing but JS renderer stalled) would show a frozen UI with no watchdog to catch it.
EVIDENCE: src/app/api/market/vector/stream/route.ts:136-143 (15s comment heartbeat); src/app/api/market/zerodte/marks/stream/route.ts:129-133 (same); src/app/api/market/spx/pulse/stream/route.ts:174-181 (same, with explicit comment explaining purpose); src/app/api/market/stocks/spot-stream/route.ts:113-118 (same); src/app/api/market/flows/stream/route.ts:113-115 (25s JSON data heartbeat, different mechanism); src/lib/api.ts:893-950 (no independent heartbeat watchdog; reconnect only on native onerror).

**CQ-124** | PROVEN
A: Both tabs are allowed, and Vector's cap counts toward a single GLOBAL total, not a per-user budget. `tryAcquireVectorStreamConnection(maxStreams)` checks/increments one module-level counter `totalStreams` with no userId parameter and no per-user map anywhere in the hub file. `MAX_STREAMS = Number(process.env.SSE_MAX_STREAMS ?? 2000)` is a single cap per ECS replica shared by every connection regardless of who owns it. `streamUserId` derived in the route is used only for the entitlement recheck, never for connection accounting. So two tabs (or N tabs/tickers) from one user consume N of the 2000 global slots exactly like N different users would, with no per-user throttle — a billing-fairness gap the question correctly flags.
EVIDENCE: src/features/vector/lib/vector-stream-hub.ts:121-129 (tryAcquireVectorStreamConnection/releaseVectorStreamConnection operate on module-level `totalStreams`, no userId param); src/app/api/market/vector/stream/route.ts:23 (`MAX_STREAMS = Number(process.env.SSE_MAX_STREAMS ?? 2000)`), :43-45 (acquire call, no user context passed), :49 (`streamUserId` used only later for recheckSseUserEntitlement, line 76).

**CQ-125** | PROVEN
A: No sequence numbers are enforced on Vector's SSE stream and there is no client-side gap-detection/resync request path. The frame is built as plain `data: ${JSON.stringify(payload)}\n\n` with no `id:` line and no seq/sequence field anywhere in the TickerHub type or payload construction. By contrast, the ADMIN api-live-feed stream DOES implement Last-Event-ID/seq-based resume server- and client-side, proving the codebase has the pattern available and simply did not apply it to Vector. Vector instead relies on an idempotent merge: the first frame (`latestFullFrame`) carries the whole wallHistory, steady-state frames (`latestDeltaFrame`) carry only the newest tail sample, and the client unions samples via `mergeWallHistory` — this masks small history-array gaps harmlessly (one fewer sample merged) but there is zero detection for a dropped/coalesced tick affecting the non-history fields (candle, walls, vexWalls, gammaFlip, darkPoolLevels); the client cannot know one was missed, let alone request a resync.
EVIDENCE: src/features/vector/lib/vector-stream-hub.ts:9-26 (TickerHub type, no seq field), :46-47 (`frame()` = plain `data: ...\n\n`, no `id:`), :60-66 (full vs delta frame construction, tail-only delta); src/app/api/admin/apis/stream/route.ts:28-31 (`Last-Event-ID` header read, `sinceSeq` parsed) contrasted with src/components/admin/AdminApiLiveFeed.tsx:218-220 (client tracks lastEventId/seq) — confirming the pattern exists elsewhere in the repo but is absent from Vector.

**CQ-126** | PROVEN
A: Confirmed and diffed directly: zerodte/marks/stream includes a real 'nighthawk' tool-launch gate at BOTH connect time (`requireToolApi("nighthawk")`) and on every SSE recheck (`recheckSseUserEntitlement(streamUserId, "premium", "nighthawk")`), and passing a `tool` argument makes `recheckSseUserEntitlement` additionally call `userCanAccessTool(userId, tool)`, failing closed if that specific launch gate fails independent of tier. vector/stream is symmetric with the 'vector' tool key (`requireToolApi("vector")` at connect, `recheckSseUserEntitlement(streamUserId,"premium","vector")` on tick). flows/stream, by contrast, has NO tool-launch gate anywhere — connect-time auth is only `authorizePremiumDeskApi` plus a flows-specific SSE rate limit, and its recheck call omits the third argument entirely: `recheckSseUserEntitlement(streamUserId, "premium")`, i.e. tier-only. So there is a confirmed inconsistency: Vector and Night Hawk marks streams enforce a live tool-launch recheck; Flows enforces tier only. Whether that's intentional (no distinct launch-gated 'flows' ToolKey exists) or a genuine gap needs one more check — src/lib/tool-access.ts's ToolKey enum — to see if a 'flows' tool key even exists to omit; not checked in this pass.
EVIDENCE: src/app/api/market/zerodte/marks/stream/route.ts:41 (`requireToolApi("nighthawk")` connect-time), :79 (`recheckSseUserEntitlement(streamUserId, "premium", "nighthawk")`); src/app/api/market/vector/stream/route.ts:29 (`requireToolApi("vector")`), :76 (`recheckSseUserEntitlement(streamUserId, "premium", "vector")`); src/app/api/market/flows/stream/route.ts:23 (`authorizePremiumDeskApi` only, no requireToolApi call anywhere in file), :65 (`recheckSseUserEntitlement(streamUserId, "premium")` — no tool arg); src/lib/sse-stream-entitlement.ts:16,21-24 (`tool?: ToolKey` optional param, only invokes `userCanAccessTool` when passed).

## O. Architecture

**CQ-127** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: The specific mechanism named in the question is DISPROVEN: ensureDataSockets() does not block or cause latency on web tier's first market API hit — it's idempotent and everything expensive is fire-and-forget. But the underlying concern (real cold-start latency after deploy) IS real via a different mechanism: awaitWebBootWarm() races boot-warm work against a hard 2.5s sleep cap and returns regardless of outcome, so if bootstrap/GEX/board warming takes longer than 2.5s on a cold cache, ECS can mark the task ready before warming finishes, and the first member request on an under-warmed path pays a cold chain-fetch penalty.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/process-role.ts:33-36 (shouldBootDataSockets false on web tier); src/lib/ws/init-data-sockets.ts:41-76 ('web tier — skipping upstream WS boot' log, RTH warm leader / bead recorder both fired via `void import(...).catch(...)`, never awaited); src/lib/ws/uw-socket.ts:1213-1231 (ensureUwClusterFreshnessPoller is non-blocking: void + setInterval); src/lib/web-boot-warm.ts:29-73 (ensureWebBootWarm fires 5 concurrent warms; awaitWebBootWarm does `Promise.race([bootWarmInflight, sleep(maxMs=2_500)])` and returns either way; comment: 'so the first member poll after a deploy hits primed in-memory mirrors instead of a cold chain fetch'); src/app/api/ready/route.ts:17-19 (calls ensureWebBootWarm()+awaitWebBootWarm() from the ECS readiness gate, comment: 'ECS deploy gate'). Live check that would prove/disprove the residual risk: AWS/ApplicationELB TargetResponseTime p99/Max on blackout-production-app's target group in the ~60s window immediately after a rolling deploy, cross-referenced with `/ecs/blackout-production` CloudWatch Logs grepped for '[web-boot-warm] non-fatal' timestamps — not run this pass (no live AWS creds).

**CQ-128** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: No single page in docs/ names 'gamma flip / spot price / flow alerts / earnings dates → this exact module is the source of truth.' The closest artifact, LARGO-PRODUCT-CONTRACT.md, standardizes the SHAPE every value must carry to Largo, not which module computes each metric. Concrete evidence of drift: gamma flip alone has at least two conceptually different implementations in the repo with no doc distinguishing which is canonical for which consumer.
EVIDENCE: grep across docs/*.md and docs/**/*.md for 'canonical'/'source of truth' found many hits but no dedicated per-metric registry doc; docs/audit/LARGO-PRODUCT-CONTRACT.md ('The governing principle' section) defines C1-C8 contract fields, not per-metric ownership; src/lib/research/gamma-levels-core.ts:233 computes a distinct research-only `medianFlip(input.samples)`; src/lib/providers/gex-positioning.ts:326 reads `gammaFlip: base.flip` off a shared computeGexWalls-style object; src/lib/meridian/meridian-viz-core.ts:420 reads `thermal.flip` from an already-computed Thermal value rather than recomputing — i.e. at least 2 distinct 'gamma flip' definitions coexist (live posture flip vs. research median-flip) with no page stating which is authoritative for a given cross-product question. This matches the repo's own stated design philosophy ('cross-product disagreement is represented, never reconciled', CLAUDE.md Largo section) but that philosophy is not the same as a canonical-source registry, which does not exist.

**CQ-131** | PARTIALLY PROVEN

I could not find a specific past deploy-incident writeup in FINDINGS.md tying an actual outage to one of these keys in the time available.

**CQ-132** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: Postgres connection-pool exhaustion is the strongest evidence-based candidate for 'fails first at 10x users,' because the codebase already anticipates and warns about it explicitly: a fixed PgBouncer backend budget (default 20) is divided across N ECS web replicas, and the code's own comment gives a concrete overshoot example (15-per-task pool x 5 replicas = 75 against a 20 budget). UW's rate limiter is a hard, replica-independent plan cap (120/min) that scales with distinct-ticker/feature fan-out rather than directly with user count, so it likely degrades more gracefully unless 10x users also means 10x distinct tickers requested. No equivalent capacity-math constant was found in this pass for Redis memory or SSE connection caps, so the ranking cannot be fully closed without live CloudWatch/ElastiCache metrics.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/db.ts:32-38 (computeSafePgPoolMaxDefault(pgBouncerBackendBudget, replicaCount, reservedForOtherServices) pure function), :60 (PGBOUNCER_BACKEND_BUDGET = envNumber('PGBOUNCER_DEFAULT_POOL_SIZE', 20)), :41 (REPLICA_COUNT_FOR_POOL default 1), :56 (REPLICA_COUNT_MAX_FOR_POOL, autoscaling-ceiling variant), :208-232 (runtime warning logic: 'PG_POOL_MAX=15 x 5 replicas = 75 against a 20-backend budget) — we don't silently clobber' / 'Cluster-wide Postgres connection oversubscription risk — lower PG_POOL_MAX or raise PgBouncer's pool size', plus a second check against REPLICA_COUNT_MAX_FOR_POOL for the autoscaling ceiling); src/lib/providers/uw-shared-cache.ts:3 ('staying under the 120/min UW plan cap'), lines 12-40 (per-dataset TTL table e.g. marketTide 180s, netPremTicks 60s, ivRank 3600s — call volume scales with distinct tickers/features requested, not directly with concurrent users). Live check that would fully prove/rank this: current ECS task-definition env values for PG_POOL_MAX/REPLICA_COUNT/PGBOUNCER_DEFAULT_POOL_SIZE, plus AWS/RDS DatabaseConnections and AWS/ElastiCache CurrConnections/DatabaseMemoryUsagePercentage CloudWatch metrics under real load — not pulled this pass (no live AWS creds in this session).

## P. Infrastructure / SRE

**CQ-133** | PROVEN
A: It is ECS's native ROLLING deployment (deploymentConfiguration minimumHealthyPercent/maximumPercent), not CodeDeploy blue/green — no CODE_DEPLOY controller, target-group swap, or blue/green terminology appears anywhere in the repo. `.github/workflows/ecr-push-production.yml` builds one image tagged both `:${GITHUB_SHA}` and `:latest`, then rolls two ECS services in sequence with DIFFERENT rolling parameters, and the difference is the actual split-brain mitigation: `blackout-production-web` (web tier, stateless, additive capacity) uses `minimumHealthyPercent=100, maximumPercent=120` (lines 269-274) — comments at lines 221-268 show this was deliberately raised from a prior 50/112 specifically so a new replacement task must become healthy BEFORE any old task is stopped, i.e. old and new versions run side-by-side throughout the whole rollout rather than the fleet ever dropping below desiredCount. The job polls up to 36 minutes (`for i in $(seq 1 144); sleep 15`, lines 293-342) for `rolloutState=COMPLETED`; real production timing captured in docs/audit/FINDINGS.md (~line 3060-3095) shows an 8-task fleet actually took ~26 minutes, replacing ~1 task every ~2:45 (start→register→drain-old), so two SHA versions can coexist serving live traffic for TENS OF MINUTES per deploy — this is expected/by design, not a bug, and the 'Validate static assets on origin' gate (lines 400-439) exists specifically because the mixed-version window causes real chunk-hash 404s until it fully converges. `blackout-production-market-worker` (the Redis/WS ingest tier) instead uses `minimumHealthyPercent=0, maximumPercent=200` (line 580-585) — comments at lines 541-579 explain this is the OPPOSITE tradeoff on purpose: worker capacity is NOT additive (exactly one replica holds each upstream WS at a time), so minHealth=100 would force an overlapping start where the incoming task can't win the Redis SETNX lock the outgoing task still holds, actually LENGTHENING the ingest gap. Split-brain protection for this asymmetric-overlap tier is `src/lib/ws/leader-lock-fencing.ts`: each leader holds a random per-process TOKEN (not a constant '1'), and renew/release only touch the Redis key via a Lua script that checks the token still matches before acting — this specifically closes the hole where a stalled old-version replica could blindly re-arm a lock now held by a new-version replica, which is exactly the split-brain-writer scenario the question asks about. So: web tier deliberately overlaps for the whole rollout (up to ~36 min) with NO split-brain risk because it holds no leader lock (stateless reads/API only); worker tier is fenced against split-brain by token-checked Lua scripts regardless of overlap duration. CronS (the other Redis-writer class) are separately protected per-cron by `sharedCacheSetNx` overlap guards named in CLAUDE.md's performance mandate (vector-pick-sweep etc.) — those guard against a SLOW cron overlapping its OWN next scheduled run, a different mechanism from deploy-time dual-SHA overlap, and are unrelated to this specific deploy-overlap question.
EVIDENCE: .github/workflows/ecr-push-production.yml:221-274 (web minHealthy=100/max=120, with the full arithmetic derivation in comments), :541-585 (worker minHealthy=0/max=200, leader-election rationale), :293-342 (36-min poll budget); docs/audit/FINDINGS.md ~lines 3060-3095 (measured real 26-min/8-task rollout, ~2:45/task cadence); src/lib/ws/leader-lock-fencing.ts:1-54 (fenced-token Lua scripts preventing split-brain WS leadership).

**CQ-134** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: `/api/ready` (src/app/api/ready/route.ts) is exactly what the Docker HEALTHCHECK hits (`deploy/Dockerfile`: `HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=5s --start-period=90s --retries=3 CMD ... fetch('http://127.0.0.1:.../api/ready')`), and this is the exact 90s figure named in the question — confirmed identically in a live `describe_task_definition` capture recorded in docs/audit/FINDINGS.md line 3095 (`interval: 30s, timeout: 5s, retries: 3, startPeriod: 90s`). During the 90s window (and beyond, until it passes) the route does two things that can fail/delay it: (1) `awaitWebBootWarm(2500ms)` (src/lib/web-boot-warm.ts:70-74) — capped at 2.5s, so it cannot itself hold up readiness past that; it fires cache-priming for the bootstrap bundle, SPX desk, GEX heatmap seeds, the 0DTE board payload, and the Vector stream hub, all via `Promise.allSettled` so one failing does not fail readiness. (2) `pingDatabaseConnectivity()` retried up to 6 times at 4s intervals (`READY_ATTEMPTS=6, READY_RETRY_MS=4000`, lines 9-10, 28-42) — up to 24s of retrying a still-cold PgBouncer/RDS-proxy connection; if all 6 fail the route returns `503 { ok:false, db:'unreachable' }`. Redis is checked (`redisStatus()`) but only reported in the payload, not gating the 200/503 decision. So the checks that can genuinely fail/delay during the window are DB connectivity (gating, up to 24s of retries) and boot-warm cache priming (non-gating, 2.5s cap). Whether ANY desk actually serves 503 to a member during this window is NOT directly proven from this repo: the ECS web-tier rollout is configured `minimumHealthyPercent=100` specifically so a new task is never registered with the ALB target group until its container health check (i.e. /api/ready) passes (ecr-push-production.yml:221-232's own comment: 'a deploy can only ever ADD capacity to a saturated fleet, never subtract it') — meaning under normal single-service rolling deploys, a cold/unready replica should never receive live traffic and thus never itself serve a 503 to a member. A real historical incident (docs/audit/FINDINGS.md line ~20057, 2026-07-30 finding) shows the PRIOR failure mode was subtly different and worse than a 503: '/api/ready returned before ensureWebBootWarm finished — ALB admitted cold replicas' — i.e. before the awaitWebBootWarm fix, a replica that passed the (then-unguarded) DB-only ready check was marked healthy and DID receive traffic while still cold, serving EMPTY boards/matrices rather than a 503 (fixed by adding the 2.5s-capped await). No evidence was found of any desk currently returning an explicit 503 gated on ready status; a 503 would only be expected if the ALB's own target group HealthCheckPath (which lives in blackout-infra terraform, not this repo) also points at /api/ready AND all targets simultaneously failed it, which minHealthy=100 is designed to prevent.
EVIDENCE: deploy/Dockerfile HEALTHCHECK line (start-period=90s, CMD fetches /api/ready); src/app/api/ready/route.ts:1-50 (READY_ATTEMPTS=6/READY_RETRY_MS=4000, 503 on exhaustion, redisStatus non-gating); src/lib/web-boot-warm.ts:65-75 (awaitWebBootWarm 2500ms cap); docs/audit/FINDINGS.md:3095 (live describe_task_definition confirming startPeriod:90s); docs/audit/FINDINGS.md ~20057 ('/api/ready returned before ensureWebBootWarm finished — ALB admitted cold replicas', now fixed); ecr-push-production.yml:221-232 (minHealthy=100 rationale). Live check that would fully prove/disprove the 503 half: `aws elbv2 describe-target-groups`/`describe-target-health` for `blackout-production-app` to read the ALB's own HealthCheckPath/interval, plus watching a real rolling deploy's CloudWatch ALB access logs (if ever enabled — FINDINGS notes access logging is currently OFF) for any 503 correlated with a task's `startPeriod` window.

**CQ-135** | PROVEN
A: The premise needs correcting slightly, but the underlying risk is fully confirmed: Railway itself is fully decommissioned (`.github/workflows/railway-cron-config-check.yml` and `railway-audit-apply.yml` are both headed '[DEPRECATED] ... Railway decommissioned 2026-07. All infra now on AWS ECS' and are `on: workflow_dispatch` only — no schedule, no push trigger, so they never run automatically). So there are currently NO crons running only on Railway — every cron executes via EventBridge -> a Lambda (`hit-cron`) -> the ECS `/api/cron/*` route. HOWEVER, the 49 `railway.*.toml` files (a Railway-era naming convention retained on purpose per their own comments, e.g. `railway.banger-live-sync.toml` line 1: 'Railway / EventBridge schedule catalog') are still the ACTUAL SOURCE OF TRUTH the separate `blackout-infra` repo's `sync-cron-schedules.mjs` reads to provision/update the real EventBridge rules — so a TOML file with no corresponding registry/service-map entry (or vice versa) is exactly the class of silent-schedule-loss bug PR #3940 fixed (railway.banger-live-sync.toml's own header explains: `banger-live-sync` had been hand-added to cron-jobs.json with no TOML behind it, so a routine sync run would have silently DELETED its EventBridge schedule while the route kept reporting healthy, with `produces_member_alert=true` meaning a dead job stops live banger-board updates with no staleness alarm). Running the repo's own drift detector (`node scripts/validate-railway-cron-manifest.mjs`) RIGHT NOW on this checkout reproduces exactly this failure class, unfixed: `registry=48 toml=49 services=42`, with concrete drift — `banger-discovery`, `banger-live-sync`, `darkpool-discord`, `helix-discord-digest`, `thermal-discord`, and `x-analytics` are all in the cron registry but MISSING from `CRON_SERVICE_NAMES` (the service map); `x-analytics` also has no TOML at all; and `railway.thermal-discord-breach.toml` / `railway.x-intel.toml` exist with NO registry entry. So yes — the manifest CAN drift silently today, because (a) the validator that would catch it (`validate-railway-cron-manifest.mjs`) is wired into workflows that are ALL manual-dispatch-only (`railway-cron-config-check.yml`, `railway-audit-apply.yml`, `railway-provision.yml`), never run on a schedule or on push, and (b) it is currently failing with 10 real drift issues that nobody has acted on. `docs/audit/FINDINGS.md` (2026-09-04 entry, ~line 3117) independently documents a related live case: three Discord-digest crons (`darkpool-discord`, `thermal-discord`, `helix-discord-digest`) were confirmed live/ENABLED/firing in production via `describe_rule` while being ABSENT from `CRON_JOBS` — meaning the admin cron-health board and `cron-staleness-watchdog` never evaluated them at all; that specific gap was partially fixed (all three were added to CRON_JOBS on 2026-09-04) but they still show up in the CRON_SERVICE_NAMES drift above, so the fix was incomplete.
EVIDENCE: railway.banger-live-sync.toml:1-10 (PR #3940's own header explaining the exact silent-deletion mechanism); .github/workflows/railway-cron-config-check.yml:1-9 and railway-audit-apply.yml:1-3 ('DEPRECATED ... Retained for reference only; triggers disabled', `on: workflow_dispatch` only); `node scripts/validate-railway-cron-manifest.mjs` run live in this session: `registry=48 toml=49 services=42` with 10 FAIL lines listing the exact missing/orphaned keys named above; docs/audit/FINDINGS.md ~line 3117-3126 (2026-09-04, three live Discord crons found invisible to CRON_JOBS/staleness watchdog, FIXED same day but still showing as CRON_SERVICE_NAMES drift in the current run).

However, adjacent audit evidence strongly suggests `gex-heatmap` is the likely bottleneck when it triggers a cold matrix rebuild: `scripts/audit/gex-force-rebuild-timing.mjs` measured forced (`?force=1`) GEX-matrix rebuilds at p95 5.4s (SPY) / 7.3s (SPX) / 4.4s (QQQ) / 2.1s (IWM) overnight (2026-08-14, RUN-LOG) — a plain (non-force) ticker switch to an already-warm ticker should be much faster (cache hit), but a switch to a cold/rarely-viewed ticker (e.g. NVDA if not pre-warmed) could hit a similar rebuild path. `/api/market/quote` is a lightweight spot-price read and unlikely to be the bottleneck. EVIDENCE: src/features/thermal/components/GexHeatmap.tsx:2627-2645 (gex-heatmap SWR keyed on ticker), :2671-2672 (quote SWR keyed on ticker), :1458-1462 (ticker-search SWR, debounced, not switch-triggered); grep across src/features/thermal/components/*.tsx found no other ticker-keyed SWR/fetch call outside GexHeatmap.tsx; docs/audit/RUN-LOG.md 2026-08-14 gex-force-rebuild-timing.mjs run (SPY p95 5.4s / SPX 7.3s / QQQ 4.4s / IWM 2.1s, overnight — explicitly not an RTH number per the script's own caveat).

**CQ-143** | DISPROVEN
A: The premise that a per-SQL-query p95 can be named from `pg_stat_statements` is false as things currently stand: `docs/bie/FULL-SYSTEM-AWARENESS.md:144` records 'CHECKED 2026-07-03, not enabled — per explicit user instruction, checked only, never attempted to enable', and `src/lib/pg-stat-statements-health.ts` is deliberately a presence-check-only probe (`probePgStatStatements()`) that queries `pg_extension` for the extension and explicitly never builds a slow-query list ('Never fabricates a slow-query list beyond this presence check'). So there is no query-level p95 duration data available anywhere, live or in-repo. What DOES exist is `cron_job_runs` (src/lib/db.ts:1010-1023: `id, job_key, status, started_at, duration_ms, message, meta_json`, indexed on `(job_key, started_at DESC)`), populated via `logCronRun`/`recordCronJobRun` (src/lib/cron-run.ts:12-38) — but this tracks per-CRON-RUN wall-clock duration (the whole HTTP handler, including all its internal queries/upstream calls combined), not a named SQL query's own duration. So the question as asked ('name query and p95 duration') cannot be answered even from cron_job_runs — the finest granularity available is 'which cron job_key is slowest end-to-end', not 'which query'. LIVE CHECK (for the cron-level version of this question): `SELECT job_key, percentile_cont(0.95) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY duration_ms) FROM cron_job_runs WHERE started_at > now() - interval '7 days' GROUP BY job_key ORDER BY 2 DESC LIMIT 5;` against the production DB (not reachable from this sandbox — raw Postgres TCP is blocked per CLAUDE.md; would need an admin API route or ECS exec). Enabling `pg_stat_statements` for real query-level answers is an infra-level Postgres config change requiring explicit operator go-ahead per the code comment, and has not been done.
EVIDENCE: docs/bie/FULL-SYSTEM-AWARENESS.md:144; src/lib/pg-stat-statements-health.ts:1-29 (probePgStatStatements — presence-only, comment: 'Never fabricates a slow-query list beyond this presence check'); src/lib/db.ts:1010-1023 (cron_job_runs schema, only job-level duration_ms, no per-query breakdown); src/lib/cron-run.ts:12-38 (logCronRun records one duration_ms per whole cron invocation).

**CQ-144** | PROVEN
A: There is exactly one Lambda function in the whole AWS account, `blackout-production-hit-cron`, and it is NOT part of the member-facing API/page latency path at all — it is a thin EventBridge-triggered HTTP dispatcher whose only job is to `GET` each cron route on the ECS app with a Bearer `CRON_SECRET` header, then abort its own client-side fetch after a timeout (`TIMEOUT_BY_PATH[path] ?? 60_000` per FINDINGS.md:19180, function timeout 300s). Live-verified counts from a prior sweep: '0 non-terminated EC2 instances, exactly 1 Lambda function in the account, 0 EventBridge Scheduler schedules' (docs/audit/FINDINGS.md:3137). Every user-facing route (`/api/market/*`, `/dashboard`, `/nighthawk`, `/heatmap`, etc.) is served by ECS Fargate tasks (`blackout-production-web` service) behind an ALB target group (`blackout-production-app`), never by Lambda — this is explicitly stated in CLAUDE.md's environment-realities section ('All infrastructure runs on AWS ECS only ... Production is now the ONLY environment'). So the correct, explicit answer is: **there is no serverless/Lambda component in the API-request latency path a member experiences; the sole Lambda in the account is a cron-invocation dispatcher, and any cold start it incurs affects only how promptly a scheduled cron GET fires, not member-facing page/API TTFB.** A cold start on `hit-cron` could theoretically delay a cron job's *start* by however long Node cold-start init takes (typically low hundreds of ms for a small handler), but this has never been measured directly in this repo's audit history — the closest related finding is about the Lambda's 60s client-fetch abort timing out against a 300s-long cron handler (nighthawk-edition), which is an abort-timing issue, not a cold-start latency issue. LIVE CHECK: CloudWatch Logs Insights on `/aws/lambda/blackout-production-hit-cron` filtering on `@type = "REPORT"` and `@message like /Init Duration/` to pull actual cold-start `Init Duration` values, or `aws lambda get-function --function-name blackout-production-hit-cron` to confirm runtime/architecture/provisioned-concurrency settings.
EVIDENCE: docs/audit/FINDINGS.md:3137 ('...exactly 1 Lambda function in the account, 0 EventBridge Scheduler schedules'); docs/audit/FINDINGS.md:19180 (TIMEOUT_BY_PATH client-fetch budget vs 300s function timeout, blackout-infra/terraform/modules/crons/lambda/index.js:35 and crons/main.tf:68); CLAUDE.md environment-realities: 'All infrastructure runs on AWS ECS only ... Production is now the ONLY environment (blackout-production-cluster / blackout-production-web at blackouttrades.com)'; docs/audit/FINDINGS.md:3358 confirms the hit-cron Lambda's role is solely to GET cron paths (`{"key":"helix-signal-outcomes","path":"/api/cron/helix-signal-outcomes"}`) — an invoker, not a request-serving API.

## R. Observability

**CQ-145** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: There is no CloudWatch metric-alarm catalog for data freshness in this repo — there is no infrastructure/Terraform code in this repo at all. `docs/ops/AWS-MIGRATION-PLAN.md:8` and `AWS-CLEANUP-PLAN.md:7` point CloudWatch/alarm/Terraform ownership to a SEPARATE repo, `blackout-infra`, not attached to this session, so the actual alarm catalog cannot be inspected here. What DOES exist in this repo is app-level freshness alerting via Discord, not CloudWatch: (1) `src/app/api/cron/cron-staleness-watchdog/route.ts` posts to Discord when a job is `stale`/`failed`, with an escalated `market_hours_stale` case for live-data breaks during RTH (comment lines 18-35 name the '#90 outage' this closes); (2) `src/lib/public-gex-snapshot.ts:60-88` `noteWarmingAndMaybeAlarm()`/`shouldAlarmPublicGexWarming()` with `PUBLIC_GEX_WARMING_ALARM_SEC = 300` (`src/lib/public-gex-snapshot-types.ts:253`) fires `captureError(...)` (goes to `error_events` + optional Sentry) when a GEX snapshot sits 'warming' >300s. Neither is a CloudWatch alarm and neither carries a coded P0/P1 label — severity is ad hoc strings ('critical'/'warning'). "How many fired in the last 30 days" is UNKNOWN from static code.
EVIDENCE: src/app/api/cron/cron-staleness-watchdog/route.ts:18-35 (rationale + RTH escalation), :249-263 (notifyOpsDiscord call + 'ALERT NOT DELIVERED' fallback log); src/lib/public-gex-snapshot.ts:60-88 (noteWarmingAndMaybeAlarm); src/lib/public-gex-snapshot-types.ts:253 (PUBLIC_GEX_WARMING_ALARM_SEC=300); docs/ops/AWS-MIGRATION-PLAN.md:8, docs/ops/AWS-CLEANUP-PLAN.md:7 (infra lives in blackout-infra, not this repo). LIVE CHECK TO PROVE FIRING COUNT: (a) AWS account — aws cloudwatch describe-alarms --alarm-name-prefix blackout-production plus describe-alarm-history over 30 days for any freshness-named alarm; (b) query error_events for source='manual' AND message LIKE 'Public GEX snapshot warming%' over 30 days via GET /api/admin/errors (admin-gated); (c) pull ops Discord channel history for cron-staleness-watchdog posts over 30 days.

**CQ-149** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: The error_events schema is id BIGSERIAL, source TEXT, scope TEXT, name TEXT, message TEXT, stack TEXT, meta_json JSONB, created_at TIMESTAMPTZ — there is NO resolved/status/acknowledged column of any kind. This DISPROVES the 'marked resolved without fix' half of the question as literally asked: nothing can be marked resolved because that state doesn't exist in the schema. The only lifecycle event a row has is capacity-based pruning once older than the newest ERROR_EVENTS_KEEP=2000 rows — deletion, not resolution-tracking. Errors ARE grouped for alerting: countRecentErrorEvents() runs `SELECT source, scope, COUNT(*) ... GROUP BY source, scope ORDER BY COUNT(*) DESC LIMIT 8` over a configurable window (used by the cron watchdog's error-spike alert, default 15m), but no admin surface runs this over a 7-day window — GET /api/admin/errors only returns raw newest-N rows, ungrouped. 'Top 3 signatures last 7 days' is answerable in principle with the same SQL shape (sinceMinutes=10080, LIMIT 3) but I cannot execute it here — raw Postgres TCP is blocked from this sandbox per this repo's own documented environment limitation.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/db.ts:1130-1139 (CREATE TABLE error_events schema, no resolved/status column); src/lib/error-sink.ts:47 (ERROR_EVENTS_KEEP=2000), :113-145 (persistErrorEvent + opportunistic prune-by-capacity, no resolution logic), :246-268 (countRecentErrorEvents: SELECT source, scope, COUNT(*) FROM error_events ... GROUP BY source, scope ORDER BY COUNT(*) DESC LIMIT 8), :151-172 (dbQueryCapturedErrors WeakSet dedup guard — documents a real double-counting risk across (source, scope), the closest thing to 'noise' in this system); src/app/api/admin/errors/route.ts:16-20 (fetchRecentErrorEvents(limit), ungrouped raw rows, admin-gated). LIVE CHECK: run `SELECT source, scope, COUNT(*) FROM error_events WHERE created_at > NOW() - INTERVAL '7 days' GROUP BY source, scope ORDER BY COUNT(*) DESC LIMIT 3;` via an ECS exec session or a temporary debug endpoint (raw TCP to Postgres is blocked from this sandbox).
