## S. Security

**CQ-150** | PROVEN
A: No single check named `isPublicMutationRoute` coverage catches a new /api/market/ POST route added incorrectly — but the risk splits into two independent mechanisms with two independent (and incomplete) tests, and the question's framing conflates them. (1) `scripts/verify-api-auth-guards.mjs` (wired as `npm run validate:api-auth`, and run directly in CI: `.github/workflows/ci.yml:53` `node scripts/verify-api-auth-guards.mjs`) is a static source-scan of every `route.ts` under `src/app/api` that requires one of GUARD_PATTERNS (`requireTierApi`, `authorizeMarketDeskApi`, `authorizePremiumDeskApi`, `isCronAuthorized`, etc., lines 79-92) OR membership in `PUBLIC_ROUTE_ALLOWLIST`. This DOES catch a new `/api/market/*` route.ts that forgets to call any tier/auth guard helper — exit code 1, listed by path. (2) `isPublicMutationRoute` (src/middleware-clerk.ts:61-68) is a completely separate, hand-maintained middleware allowlist of routes exempt from the blanket 'POST/PUT/PATCH/DELETE to /api/* needs a Bearer token or Clerk cookie' guard (middleware-clerk.ts:148-165). The code's own comment (lines 43-59) states explicitly: 'a route being public in its own code is not enough... A public POST route that is not listed here returns 401 to exactly the audience it exists for, and nothing in CI catches it: the guard scan passes, tsc passes, unit tests pass' — this is literally describing the historical email-capture 401 bug named in the question's WHY. The ONE test that does check the isPublicMutationRoute list (`src/middleware-public-mutation.test.ts`, 'every public route with a mutation handler is in the exemption list') only walks `src/app/api/public/**` (line 40: `walk(join(API_ROOT, 'public'))`) — it never scans `src/app/api/market/**`. So: (a) a new /api/market/ POST route missing a tier-guard call IS caught by validate:api-auth; (b) if a new /api/market/ POST route were ever meant to be a public (logged-out) mutation and the author forgot to add it to isPublicMutationRoute, NOTHING in CI catches that (neither validate:api-auth, which doesn't know about middleware, nor middleware-public-mutation.test.ts, which only scans api/public/**). In practice this second case is low-risk because /api/market/ routes are never designed to be public/logged-out mutations (they gate on tier), so the gap is theoretical for that specific path prefix — but it is a real, named, uncovered gap for ANY future public POST route placed outside api/public/**.
EVIDENCE: scripts/verify-api-auth-guards.mjs:79-130 (GUARD_PATTERNS + allowlist + exit(1) on failure); .github/workflows/ci.yml:53 (`node scripts/verify-api-auth-guards.mjs`); package.json:152 (`"validate:api-auth": "node scripts/verify-api-auth-guards.mjs"`); src/middleware-clerk.ts:43-68,148-165 (isPublicMutationRoute + its own comment describing the exact blind spot); src/middleware-public-mutation.test.ts:27-42 (`walk(join(API_ROOT, "public"))` — only scans api/public/**, never api/market/**).

**CQ-151** | DISPROVEN
A: The premise as literally stated is false: `/api/market/largo/context` (src/app/api/market/largo/context/route.ts) takes a `ticker` query param, not `sessionId` — it has no notion of a stored Largo session at all; it's a stateless market-data rail (spot/gex walls/flow net) gated only by `requireTierApi("premium")`, identical for any premium user regardless of identity. The actual session-bearing endpoint is `/api/market/largo/session` (GET/PATCH, src/app/api/market/largo/session/route.ts), which DOES take `session_id` and IS IDOR-hardened: `getLargoSessionMessages(sessionId, authResult.userId)` and `fetchLargoSessionMetadata(sessionId, authResult.userId)` both pass the authenticated caller's own userId (from `requireTierApi`, never client-supplied) down to src/lib/largo/largo-store.ts, which enforces real ownership: `fetchLargoSessionMetadata` runs `SELECT metadata FROM largo_sessions WHERE id = $1 AND user_id = $2` (largo-store.ts:61-64) and separately calls `sessionOwnedByUser` first (line 60); `getLargoSessionMessages` (largo-terminal.ts:424-432) checks `sessionOwnedByUser(sid, userId)` and returns an EMPTY message list `{ session_id: sid, messages: [] }` rather than another user's data if ownership fails. There is also a defense-in-depth check for the in-memory (no-DB) fallback path: `fetchLargoMessagesPublic` (largo-store.ts:175-190) checks `memorySessionOwners.get(sessionId)` against the caller's userId and returns `[]` on mismatch, with an explicit code comment citing a prior finding: 'Owner check parity with fetchLargoHistory — without it, a premium user could read another user's in-memory conversation in no-DB mode (LARGO-10)'. So userA supplying userB's session_id gets `{messages: []}`/empty metadata, never userB's content — this class of IDOR was found and fixed previously (LARGO-10) and is now covered on both the DB and in-memory code paths.
EVIDENCE: src/app/api/market/largo/context/route.ts:38-48 (ticker param, no session_id, only requireTierApi); src/app/api/market/largo/session/route.ts:13-37 (GET passes authResult.userId, not client input); src/lib/largo/largo-store.ts:55-67 (`WHERE id = $1 AND user_id = $2`), 96-100 (sessionOwnedByUser), 175-190 (in-memory owner check + LARGO-10 comment); src/lib/largo-terminal.ts:424-432 (getLargoSessionMessages returns empty on ownership failure).

**CQ-152** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: Live-checked today (2026-09-05): `curl -sI https://blackouttrades.com/` returns a `content-security-policy` header that is BYTE-IDENTICAL to `baseCsp` in next.config.mjs:9, so there is currently NO drift between the app's own CSP header and what production actually serves — the Cloudflare Transform Rule mirror described in docs/CLOUDFLARE_CONFIG.md:64 ('Mirrors baseCsp in next.config.mjs — must stay in sync') is in sync as of this check. However: (1) the doc itself records a prior real drift incident (2026-08-03: Cloudflare transform rule missing GA domains, live-fixed via ruleset `2f92b7412d1e40c0897ccd6d082810bc`) — so drift HAS happened before and there is no automated CI check that re-verifies the two stay in sync going forward (only the manual `npm run validate:ga4` probe is named, and that checks GA collection, not full CSP parity). (2) Separately and more importantly: the shipped baseCsp ITSELF already contains `script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline' 'unsafe-eval' ...` — 'unsafe-inline' inherently permits inline `<script>` execution (defeating the core XSS mitigation CSP exists for) REGARDLESS of any drift; this is a standing, intentional weakening (needed for TradingView/GA/inline widgets), not a drift artifact, but it does mean 'any inline script injection allowed' is already TRUE by design, independent of sync status.
EVIDENCE: Live curl 2026-09-05: `content-security-policy: default-src 'self'; script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline' 'unsafe-eval' https://s.tradingview.com ...` (verbatim match to next.config.mjs:9 baseCsp string); next.config.mjs:8-9; docs/CLOUDFLARE_CONFIG.md:64,66 (sync requirement + 2026-08-03 drift incident + fix ruleset id).

**CQ-153** | DISPROVEN
A: Admin status is NOT self-assignable via any member-facing API. `isAdminUser` (src/lib/admin-access.ts:24-38) checks, in order: (1) a `role` claim on the Clerk session JWT (`adminFromJwtRole`, only trusted if the JWT itself carries `role: 'admin'|'member'` — this claim is configured server-side in the Clerk Dashboard JWT template, not user-writable), (2) `user.publicMetadata.role === 'admin'` via a memoized Clerk Backend API `getUser` call, (3) an `ADMIN_EMAILS` env-var allowlist match on the primary verified email. Clerk's `publicMetadata` (as opposed to `unsafeMetadata`) can ONLY be written server-side via the Clerk Backend API — there is no client SDK method or route in this codebase that lets a signed-in user PATCH their own publicMetadata. The only code paths that write `publicMetadata: { role: ... }` are in `src/app/api/admin/users/[id]/route.ts:152`, and that entire route (both the read and write handlers) is gated behind `resolveAdminApi()` (lines 28, 93, 226), which itself calls `isAdminUser` — i.e. only an existing admin can grant admin to another user; there is no bootstrap/self-elevation path. A user cannot forge the JWT role claim either, since it's Clerk-signed and verified server-side by `auth()`/Clerk middleware, not read from client-controlled input.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/admin-access.ts:24-38 (isAdminUser logic), 91-108 (requireAdminApi/getAdminApiActor gated by resolveAdminApi); src/lib/admin-from-jwt.ts (adminFromJwtRole — pure JWT claim check, no I/O); src/app/api/admin/users/[id]/route.ts:28,93,152,226 (role writes gated by resolveAdminApi before any publicMetadata mutation); no grep hits for a non-admin route writing publicMetadata.role.

**CQ-154** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: `POST /api/membership/sync` (src/app/api/membership/sync/route.ts:12-66) carries NO application-level CSRF token — it authenticates purely via `auth()` (Clerk's `__session` cookie / bearer), takes no request body at all, and performs no origin/referer check. Its only abuse mitigation is a per-user Redis cooldown (`acquireMembershipSyncSlot`, 429 on repeat) which limits frequency but does not prevent a single cross-site-triggered call. No app-wide CSRF middleware, token, or double-submit-cookie pattern exists anywhere in the codebase (grep for csrf/CSRF/SameSite across src turns up only TikTok OAuth `state` and the native-oauth bridge's own cookie — nothing protecting member-facing mutation routes generally). The de facto protection is Clerk's session cookie `SameSite` attribute: if it is `Lax` (Clerk's common default), a cross-site auto-submitting POST form would NOT carry the `__session` cookie (Lax only forwards cookies on top-level GET navigation, not POST), which would make classic form-based CSRF against this endpoint ineffective — but I could not directly observe the `Set-Cookie: __session=...; SameSite=...` attribute from this sandbox (a plain GET to /sign-in returns no Set-Cookie; the cookie is minted by Clerk's own FAPI domain during the actual sign-in flow, requiring a live authenticated session to inspect).
EVIDENCE: src/app/api/membership/sync/route.ts:12-25 (auth() only, no CSRF token, Redis cooldown only); repo-wide grep for csrf/CSRF/SameSite (no app-level CSRF middleware found, only OAuth-state and native-oauth cookie usages). LIVE CHECK THAT WOULD PROVE IT: authenticate a real session (mintClerkPremiumSession per data-validator.mjs), then `curl -v -b "$COOKIES" https://clerk.blackouttrades.com/...` during the FAPI ticket exchange and inspect the `Set-Cookie: __session=...` line for its `SameSite` value directly, or attempt an actual cross-origin POST with the session cookie attached via a browser test page to confirm the cookie is/isn't sent.

**CQ-155** | PROVEN
A: There is NO rate limiting today, at either the edge or the app layer, on Clerk sign-in. docs/CLOUDFLARE_CONFIG.md explicitly documents this as a known, unaddressed gap: a table titled 'Requires Pro Plan ($20/mo) — Not Yet Configured' (line 143) lists 'Rate limiting on /sign-in (10 req/min) | Brute force protection on auth' (line 147) and 'Bot Fight Mode | Blocks credential stuffing bots' (line 149) as NOT YET CONFIGURED, and line 8 confirms Cloudflare is on the Free plan ('upgrade to Pro for rate limiting + Bot Fight Mode'). The app itself has no rate-limit logic that would apply here either: `src/lib/ip-rate-limit.ts` (`checkIpRateLimit`) is a real, Redis-backed (fail-open with in-memory fallback) per-IP limiter, but it is only invoked from specific app-owned public POST routes (email-capture, gex-snapshot, etc.) — the actual credential-check request on sign-in goes directly to Clerk's own hosted FAPI (`clerk.blackouttrades.com`), which this app's middleware/routes never see, so app-level rate limiting cannot apply to it regardless. The only real limiting is Clerk's own backend rate limit, referenced informally in CLAUDE.md as 'rapid sign-in/token cycles get FAPI-rate-limited' — an externally-imposed limit whose exact requests/min threshold is Clerk's internal policy, not something this repo controls or documents a number for.
EVIDENCE: docs/CLOUDFLARE_CONFIG.md:8, 143-153 (Pro-plan-only rate limiting explicitly marked 'Not Yet Configured', naming /sign-in 10 req/min as the specific gap); src/lib/ip-rate-limit.ts:1-45 (app-level limiter exists but only wraps app-owned public routes, not Clerk's FAPI). LIVE CHECK THAT WOULD PROVE THE EXACT BRUTE-FORCE CEILING: script N rapid POSTs to Clerk's sign-in verification endpoint (via clerk.blackouttrades.com FAPI) with a disposable test account and record at what request count Clerk itself starts returning 429.

**CQ-157** | PROVEN
A: Yes — raw Clerk user emails ARE written unredacted to application logs (console.*, which ship to CloudWatch `/ecs/blackout-production` in production per this repo's own ops notes), though NOT to the durable `error_events` DB sink. Three confirmed live instances: (1) `src/lib/membership.ts:359` — `console.warn(\`[membership-reconcile] ${email} attempt ${attempt}:\`, err)` on a Whop sync retry failure; (2) `src/app/api/webhooks/clerk/route.ts:65` — `console.error(\`[clerk-webhook] Whop sync failed for ${clerkUserId} (${email}):\`, err)`; (3) same file, line 141 — `console.log(\`[clerk-webhook] Provisioned user: ${userId} (${email})\`)` on every new-user provision, which is not even an error path — it's routine info logging. None of these route through `captureError`/`error-sink.ts`'s `persistErrorEvent`, which DOES sanitize via `sanitizeTelemetrySnippet` (src/lib/api-telemetry-sanitize.ts) — but critically, that sanitizer's redaction patterns cover ONLY credential-shaped values (api_key/token/secret/password/Bearer/JWT-looking strings, lines 79-90) — there is NO email-address redaction pattern anywhere in `sanitizeTelemetrySnippet` or its siblings (`sanitizeTelemetryUrl`, `sanitizeTelemetryBody`, `sanitizeHeaderNames`), so even a future error routed through the 'redacted' sink would still leak an email embedded in a message/meta field. This is a real, unaddressed PII-in-logs gap: no redaction policy document exists for emails specifically (only credentials are covered), and CloudWatch log retention/access for `/ecs/blackout-production` is not scoped down for this class of data.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/membership.ts:359; src/app/api/webhooks/clerk/route.ts:65,141 (direct grep hits for `console.(warn|error|log)` string-interpolating `${email}`); src/lib/api-telemetry-sanitize.ts:79-90 (sanitizeTelemetrySnippet's full pattern list — credentials/tokens/JWTs only, no email regex); src/lib/error-sink.ts:113-133 (persistErrorEvent applies sanitizeTelemetrySnippet to message/stack/meta before the DB write, confirming the DB sink IS somewhat protected while plain console.* calls bypass it entirely).

## T. Auth / Entitlements / Commerce

**CQ-158** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: PR #3432 (commit dfd92d2a8, 2026-09-03) did NOT fix an 'enforcement gap' because it found there was never a code-level gate to fix — it is a NEGATIVE-RESULT audit, not a bug fix. It confirmed the copy is internally consistent (7-day money-back guarantee applies ONLY to annual Premium; SPX Slayer and monthly Premium show 'cancel anytime' with no refund, and SPX Slayer has no yearly SKU at all — plan-matrix.ts's PlanSku is only 'free'|'spx_slayer'|'premium_monthly'|'premium_yearly' and pricing.ts has no community-yearly price). Critically, the audit also found that BlackOut's own webhook handler has ZERO day-boundary/eligibility logic: `refund.created`/`refund.updated`/`dispute.created`/`dispute.updated` in src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts (lines ~358-392) call `markMembershipRevoked()` UNCONDITIONALLY regardless of how many days into the subscription the member is. So yes — as coded, if Whop itself approves a refund on day 8 (a human support decision, or a lenient/misconfigured Whop refund-window setting), BlackOut's webhook will happily revoke entitlement and process it with no complaint, while the site still advertises '7-day money-back guarantee'. Whether this actually happens depends entirely on WHOP's own refund-window enforcement (dashboard setting or manual support review), which lives outside this codebase and is unverifiable from code — I confirmed via `git log` that no commits since 09-03 touched whop-revocation.ts or the webhook route to add day-boundary logic, so the gap (if Whop-side enforcement is ever lax) remains open today. LIVE CHECK THAT WOULD PROVE IT: a human with Whop dashboard access checking the yearly-Premium product's configured refund-window setting, or submitting a real day-8 refund request and observing whether Whop support/dashboard rejects it.
EVIDENCE: src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts:358-392 (unconditional markMembershipRevoked on refund/dispute, no date check); docs/audit/findings-staging/2026-09-03-refund-guarantee-copy-verified-whop-external.md (folded from PR #3432, commit dfd92d2a8); src/lib/plan-matrix.ts:5,19-34 (no community-yearly SKU); src/app/(marketing)/refund-policy/page.tsx:35-41 (7-day annual clause); git log confirms no later commit added eligibility logic.

**CQ-159** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: Invalidation is SYNCHRONOUS for the local (replica-serving) cache and only BEST-EFFORT/fire-and-forget for cross-replica fan-out, before the 200 is returned to Whop. In src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts, on `membership.activated`/`deactivated`/`cancel_at_period_end_changed`, the handler awaits `syncWhopMembershipAndNotify(email)` (which writes Clerk publicMetadata) and then calls `for (const uid of updatedUserIds) publishTierChanged(uid);` — this line runs before the route returns. `publishTierChanged` (src/lib/tier-cache.ts:96-101) calls `invalidateTierCache(userId)` SYNCHRONOUSLY (evicts the local in-memory Map on the replica that handled the webhook, immediately), then does `void import('@/lib/redis-pubsub').then(({redisPublish}) => redisPublish(...))` — a fire-and-forget promise NOT awaited before the function/route returns, so the cross-replica Redis pub/sub broadcast to OTHER ECS replicas is not guaranteed complete before the webhook's 200 response. In practice a Redis PUBLISH is sub-millisecond-to-low-milliseconds, so the real-world propagation to peer replicas is near-instant, but it is not a hard guarantee inside this request's lifecycle. HOWEVER there is a second gap upstream of this entirely: `resolveUserTier` (tier-cache.ts:126-145) has a 'JWT fast path' — `if (fromClaims === 'premium' || fromClaims === 'community') { setTierCache(...); return fromClaims; }` — that TRUSTS the session JWT's `tier` claim without re-checking Clerk when the JWT itself still says a stale tier. For an UPGRADE this is harmless (stale JWT says 'free', code falls through to a fresh Clerk getUser per the comment 'Trust premium/community from JWT (fast path). When JWT says free, verify with Backend'), so an upgrading user is NOT stuck behind a stale free-JWT — the very next request re-verifies against Clerk directly. So for the specific upgrade-to-desk-access path asked about, the answer is: local cache eviction is synchronous and immediate; cross-replica fan-out is async but typically sub-second; and the JWT fast-path does not block upgrade recognition since it explicitly falls through on a non-premium/community claim. LIVE CHECK NEEDED to measure actual p50/p90/p95 end-to-end latency: fire a real Whop test webhook (or use a temp Clerk+Whop sandbox membership), timestamp the webhook POST, then poll a protected desk API immediately after on multiple ECS replicas (varying `X-Amzn-Trace-Id`/direct target IPs if possible) to measure wall-clock time to 200 (vs 403).
EVIDENCE: src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts:243-249 ('Evict tier cache on all replicas immediately...' comment + publishTierChanged call before route continues); src/lib/tier-cache.ts:96-101 (publishTierChanged: sync invalidateTierCache + fire-and-forget redisPublish); src/lib/tier-cache.ts:126-145 (resolveUserTier JWT fast-path, falls through to Clerk on a 'free' claim).

**CQ-160** | DISPROVEN
A: This premise is false for this codebase: there is no Discord role-sync system of any kind. Every 'discord' reference in the repo is EITHER (a) outbound incoming-webhook POSTs the app sends to Discord channels for alerts/trade notifications (thermal-discord, darkpool-discord, helix-discord-digest, ops alerts, cancellation notices, etc. — all one-way, fire-and-forget `notifyOpsDiscord`/webhook posts), or (b) a MEMBER'S OWN personal Discord webhook URL they can paste into account settings to receive alerts (`personal-alert-store.ts`, `personal-alert-validate.ts` — validates `discord.com`/`discordapp.com` `/api/webhooks/...` URLs only). There is no Discord bot token, no `DISCORD_GUILD_ID`/`DISCORD_CLIENT_ID`/OAuth integration, no code that assigns or removes a Discord GUILD ROLE based on Whop tier anywhere in src/. 'Private Discord access' is listed as a plan benefit (plan-matrix.ts) but the actual community-role grant/removal, if it happens at all, must happen entirely OUTSIDE this repo (e.g. via a Whop-Discord native integration or a manual process), and there is no reconciliation script or query here to detect role drift. A reconciliation query as asked for does not exist and cannot be built from this codebase alone.
EVIDENCE: grep across src/ for 'discord' (40+ files) shows only outbound webhook posting (src/lib/discord-post.ts, src/lib/*-discord-*.ts) and member-supplied personal alert webhooks (src/lib/personal-alert-validate.ts:5-23, accepting only discord.com/discordapp.com /api/webhooks/ URLs); no grep hit for DISCORD_BOT_TOKEN, DISCORD_GUILD_ID, DISCORD_CLIENT, discord.com/api (non-webhook), or guild/role assignment anywhere in src/.

**CQ-161** | PROVEN
A: Grace is SILENT and FULL, not a reduced/partial-access mode, and there is NO in-app UI banner — only an email. src/lib/membership.ts:139-144: `if (m.id && m.status === 'past_due' && (await isMembershipInDunningGrace(m.id))) dunningGrace.add(m.id);` then `resolveTierFromMemberships(sorted, revoked, dunningGrace)` — a membership in the grace set resolves to its ORIGINAL tier (community or premium), identically to a current paying member; there is no separate 'grace tier' or feature subset, so during the 7-day grace window (`DEFAULT_GRACE_SEC = 7 * 24 * 60 * 60` in whop-dunning.ts:18) EVERY desk the member's tier normally grants remains fully accessible, exactly as before the payment failure. The ONLY member-facing signal is an email: the webhook route (route.ts:400-408) calls `notifyPaymentFailed({ email, graceDays: dunningGraceDays() })` once per grace window (deduped via a snapshot-before-mark check so retries don't re-send). I grepped the entire src/components and src/app tree for any payment-failed/past_due/dunning-grace UI component and found NONE — no banner component references dunning state anywhere in the UI layer. So: silent full access during grace (no feature degradation), confirmed by code; and there is confirmed NO UI banner (absence proven by exhaustive grep, not just unfound).
EVIDENCE: src/lib/membership.ts:136-144; src/lib/whop-dunning.ts:18 (7-day DEFAULT_GRACE_SEC), :26-28 (dunningGraceDays exported for the email copy); src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts:400-408 (notifyPaymentFailed email dispatch, deduped); grep -rln 'past_due|dunningGrace|payment.*fail' src/components src/app --include='*.tsx' -i returned zero matches.

**CQ-162** | DISPROVEN
A: The premise misreads the product's own pricing design: SPX Slayer (community tier, $49/mo per pricing.ts) IS the paid product whose entire value proposition is the SPX 0DTE desk — there is no premium-exclusive SPX field being smuggled to community users, because SPX data was never premium-gated in the first place. src/lib/plan-matrix.ts:19-34 lists SPX Slayer's `includes` as the full SPX product: 'dealer gamma & GEX positioning', '0DTE graded plays (A-F)', 'strike-level heatmaps on SPX' — all of which /api/market/spx/desk serves. The route's own auth gate, src/lib/market-api-auth.ts:74-89, has an explicit doc-comment history noting a PAST version of this exact bug class: 'Twenty premium routes... were wired to this community gate, letting a $49 community member pull $199 premium data by hitting the API directly (CWE-863)' — but that was fixed by moving those 20 routes (HELIX flows, Thermal heatmap, all of Vector, premium briefs) to `authorizePremiumDeskApi` (premium-only). `/api/market/spx/desk` itself deliberately stayed on the community gate (`authorizeMarketDeskApi` → `authorizeCronOrTierApi(req, 'community')`) because SPX IS the community-tier product. I also checked SpxDashboard.tsx for any field-level (not just page-level) tier gating within the SPX UI itself and found only ONE tier check (line 189), gating the entire page for FREE-tier users only (`tier !== 'premium' && tier !== 'community' && tier !== 'admin'`) — no per-field premium-only hiding inside the SPX desk exists to compare against the API. So there is no field-level parity gap to enumerate; the question's premise (that SPX has premium-only-in-UI fields) is false for this product.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/plan-matrix.ts:19-34 (SPX Slayer plan includes full SPX feature set); src/lib/market-api-auth.ts:74-89 (authorizeMarketDeskApi doc-comment explaining the CWE-863 history and the fix that moved OTHER products off this gate, confirming SPX correctly stays on it); src/features/spx/components/SpxDashboard.tsx:189 (only page-level free-tier gate, no field-level premium gating found via grep).

**CQ-163** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: Direct 'JWT manipulation' in the sense of a free user editing their own token's `tool_access` claim value is NOT viable: sessionClaims in this app come exclusively from Clerk's own `clerkAuth()` (src/lib/auth-server.ts:29-34), which cryptographically verifies the session JWT's signature server-side against Clerk's key before the app ever sees the claims — a client cannot forge or alter a claim without invalidating the signature, so #3906-style 'tampering' isn't directly reachable this way (#3906, per CQ-121/CQ-187 context in .blackout-agent/, was actually about STALE-but-legitimately-issued claims persisting past a real entitlement change on SSE streams, not signature forgery). The real question is whether tool-access-server.ts's JWT fast-path (`toolAccessModeFromSessionClaims` in clerk-session-claims.ts:41-49, consumed at tool-access-server.ts:36-45) could grant Vector access off a STALE `tool_access` claim after a server-side revocation, the same class of bug #3906 fixed for SSE. Evidence strongly suggests this fast-path is currently DEAD CODE in production: docs/clerk/SESSION-TOKEN-CLAIMS.md documents the ONLY two Dashboard-configured session-token claims as `tier` and `role` (`{{user.public_metadata.tier}}`, `{{user.public_metadata.role}}}`) — `tool_access` is not listed as configured anywhere, and scripts/validate-clerk-config.mjs (the script that verifies live Clerk JWT config) only checks `hasTier`/`hasRole`, never `tool_access`. If `tool_access` is genuinely never added to Clerk's session token template, then `sessionClaimsHaveToolAccessField` (clerk-session-claims.ts:33-36) always returns false in production, `toolAccessModeFromSessionClaims` always returns null, and `userCanAccessTool` (tool-access-server.ts:33-48) always falls through to `loadUserToolAccess` → a fresh (per-request-cached only) Clerk `getUser()` call — meaning no staleness window exists for tool_access specifically today. HOWEVER I found a closely-related, definitely-live staleness gap in the SIBLING mechanism `resolveUserTier` (tier-cache.ts:136-140): 'Trust premium/community from JWT (fast path)... setTierCache(userId, fromClaims); return fromClaims;' — this trusts a stale-but-signed `tier` claim (which IS configured, per the docs above) even when the server-side tier cache was just invalidated by `publishTierChanged`, because invalidation only clears the in-memory Map; the very next call re-populates it from the same stale JWT if the client hasn't refreshed its token yet. This is a genuine downgrade/revocation-lag vector analogous to #3906, but for the tier-gate rather than the tool_access gate specifically, and is the subject of my CQ-164 answer. VERDICT for the literal question asked (tool_access JWT fast-path): PARTIALLY PROVEN NOT EXPLOITABLE TODAY (code path likely inert because tool_access isn't a configured JWT claim), but this is inferred from documentation/validator scope, not a live Clerk Dashboard read. LIVE CHECK: call Clerk Backend API `GET /v1/instance` or inspect the Dashboard's Sessions → Customize session token JSON directly (requires Clerk secret key with instance-read scope) to confirm `tool_access` is absent from the template; alternatively, mint a real tool_access-granted user, decode their live session JWT (base64 payload) and check for a `tool_access` key.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/auth-server.ts:29-34 (clerkAuth() server-verifies JWT signature); src/lib/tool-access-server.ts:36-45 (JWT fast-path consumption); src/lib/clerk-session-claims.ts:33-49 (toolAccessModeFromSessionClaims / sessionClaimsHaveToolAccessField); docs/clerk/SESSION-TOKEN-CLAIMS.md (only tier+role documented as configured claims); scripts/validate-clerk-config.mjs:94-101 (validator checks only tier/role, never tool_access); src/lib/tier-cache.ts:136-140 (the analogous, confirmed-live JWT trust-on-stale-claim gap for tier).

**CQ-164** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: Revocation itself (the denylist write) is fast and durable, but propagation to an ALREADY-ISSUED session JWT is bounded by the same JWT-trust fast-path noted in CQ-163/CQ-159, not by the revocation code. On `refund.created`/`refund.updated`/`dispute.created`/`dispute.updated`, the webhook (route.ts:358-383) calls `markMembershipRevoked(membershipId)` which — per whop-revocation.ts:26-59 — writes Postgres FIRST (durable denylist, `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`) then Redis (hot cache, 400-day TTL), and only throws (triggering a Whop retry) if BOTH stores fail; then, if the payload carries the owner's email, `syncWhopMembershipForEmail(email)` re-resolves tier immediately and `publishTierChanged(uid)` evicts/broadcasts. `isMembershipRevoked` (whop-revocation.ts:65-89) is checked by `resolveMembershipTierForEmail` on every tier resolution, Redis-first then Postgres-fallback, and an hourly reconcile cron re-resolves any membership missed by the immediate path. So THE SERVER-SIDE TIER STATE is corrected essentially immediately (within one Redis write) when the chargeback webhook fires WITH an email, or within an hour via the reconcile cron if the payload lacks one (per the extractMembershipAndEmail null-email warning path documented elsewhere in the same file). The worst-case paid-access-after-chargeback window is therefore bounded by TWO independent things: (1) Whop's own latency in delivering the chargeback/dispute webhook after the actual chargeback event (entirely outside this codebase, unmeasurable from code), and (2) the SAME stale-JWT-trust gap as CQ-159/163: `resolveUserTier`'s fast path (tier-cache.ts:136-140) will keep re-trusting a member's already-issued, still-valid-looking session JWT's `tier: premium` claim for as long as that JWT is presented, REGARDLESS of the fact that the tier cache and revocation denylist have both been updated server-side — because the fast path checks the JWT claim BEFORE consulting Postgres/Redis revocation state at all (resolveUserTier never calls isMembershipRevoked directly; that check lives inside resolveMembershipTierForEmail, the sync path, not the per-request read path). This means a member with an already-cached browser session could continue to present a stale-but-cryptographically-valid premium JWT until Clerk's own client-side token refresh cycle replaces it — documented in docs/clerk/SESSION-TOKEN-CLAIMS.md as 'existing sessions refresh within ~60s' for a Dashboard CONFIG change, but that guidance is about claim-template changes, not about how fast a running browser tab re-fetches a token on its own schedule if idle. This is a real, code-confirmed worst-case exposure window whose exact duration is NOT provable from static code alone. LIVE CHECK: mint a temp premium user, capture their live `__session` JWT and decode its `exp`/iat, simulate a chargeback via a signed test Whop webhook (or ask Whop to fire one in a sandbox), then repeatedly hit a premium API endpoint with the SAME unrefreshed JWT/cookie every few seconds and record exactly when access flips to 403 — that measures the true worst-case window this analysis can only bound qualitatively.
EVIDENCE: src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts:358-392 (revocation branch); src/lib/whop-revocation.ts:26-59 (markMembershipRevoked: Postgres-first durable write, Redis hot cache, throws only on dual failure) and :65-89 (isMembershipRevoked: Redis-first, Postgres fallback, used by resolveMembershipTierForEmail's SYNC path — not by the per-request resolveUserTier read path); src/lib/tier-cache.ts:136-140 (resolveUserTier's JWT fast-path never consults the revocation denylist, only the Clerk publicMetadata tier via getClerkUserCached on a non-premium/community claim); docs/clerk/SESSION-TOKEN-CLAIMS.md ('existing sessions refresh within ~60s' — describes Dashboard config changes, not runtime token refresh cadence).

## U. CI/CD

**CQ-165** | PROVEN
A: Yes — a PR that is incorrectly undrafted before Cursor's peer-review sign-off CAN auto-merge. `automerge.yml`'s only gate is `!draft` + branch prefix (`cursor/*`/`claude/*`) + non-docs-only diff; there is no check anywhere in the workflow, in `ci.yml`'s `verify` job, or in the `cursor-merge` branch ruleset for a Cursor peer-review approval comment. The CLAUDE.md 'CARVE-OUT' requiring Cursor's sign-off before merging a self-authored PR is a purely procedural instruction to the coordinating agent, not a technical gate anywhere in code. The real safeguards that DO exist: (1) the draft flag itself, which per CLAUDE.md can only reliably be cleared via the GitHub MCP `update_pull_request(draft:false)` tool (a deliberate action, since REST PATCH silently no-ops and GraphQL/Actions-token paths are blocked) — so undrafting is a real decision point, not automatic; (2) the required `verify` CI check; (3) a docs-only-diff skip. None of these enforce review sign-off.
EVIDENCE: .github/workflows/automerge.yml:71-82 (trigger types incl. ready_for_review; sole condition `!github.event.pull_request.draft && (startsWith(github.head_ref,'cursor/')||startsWith(github.head_ref,'claude/'))`), lines 88-106 (docs-only skip), line 115 (`gh pr merge --auto --squash`), lines 42-43 comment ('GITHUB_TOKEN is sufficient for the current ruleset (0 required approvals)'), lines 35-37 comment (ruleset requires only the `verify` check). CLAUDE.md 'CARVE-OUT' section (2026-09-04) and 'THE DRAFT DEADLOCK' section (2026-08-21, undrafting mechanics).

**CQ-166** | PROVEN
A: Neither workflow's own GitHub Actions token has broad write power; both delegate all actual repo writes to an externally-launched Cursor Cloud Agent authenticated with `CURSOR_API_KEY`, whose own prompt (not the GH Actions permissions block) is the real authorization surface. `rth-autonomous-open.yml` scopes its own token to `contents: read` + `issues: write` and is schedule-disabled (manual dispatch only); its `launch-fix-agents` job only calls scripts that POST to Cursor's API. `spx-rth-all-day-agent.yml` has no `permissions:` block at all (inherits repo/org default) but also never uses GITHUB_TOKEN for a write — its only secret is CURSOR_API_KEY. That workflow's 'fix' mode prompt explicitly authorizes, without human approval: create a branch, commit code fixes, open a PR, and MERGE it, repeating until validators are green ('Branch fix/<slug>, test, PR with CODE fixes only, merge, validate:deploy... Do NOT ask the user'). Its 'verify' mode prompt explicitly forbids repo commits/docs edits except a P0 code fix PR.
EVIDENCE: .github/workflows/rth-autonomous-open.yml:14-16 (permissions: contents:read, issues:write), lines 1-2 (schedule disabled), lines 44-59 (launch-fix-agents job, CURSOR_API_KEY only). .github/workflows/spx-rth-all-day-agent.yml: zero `permissions:` block (grep confirmed), line 60 (CURSOR_API_KEY the only secret used), line 71 (fix-mode prompt: branch/test/PR/merge/repeat, no human ask), line 74 (verify-mode prompt: no commits, no docs edits, P0 code fix only).

## V. Testing

**CQ-170** | PROVEN
A: Picking the Whop webhook forged-signature scenario: there is NO regression test at all for src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts. The route's only defense against a forged webhook is `whop.webhooks.unwrap(body, { headers })` (route.ts:193), which the SDK uses to verify the Standard Webhooks signature scheme before any membership sync/tier change/revocation logic runs (route.ts:182-205). A forged or missing signature must hit the catch block and return 400 'invalid_webhook_signature' (route.ts:202-205) — but nothing in the repo exercises this path. `find`/`grep` across the whole repo (excluding node_modules/.next/worktrees) turns up zero `*.test.ts` files under src/app/api/webhook/whop/ or anywhere referencing 'webhook/whop', 'whop/route', 'WHOP_WEBHOOK_SECRET', or 'webhooks.unwrap' in a test context. Compare to the sibling Clerk webhook, which DOES have src/app/api/webhooks/clerk/route.test.ts asserting behavior for bad svix signatures. The Whop webhook drives tier sync, revocation, dunning-grace, and billing-lifecycle emails (all imported at the top of route.ts) — a forged event that slips past a broken/misconfigured signature check (or a regression that weakens it) could grant/revoke premium access with zero test to catch it. By contrast, the other two candidates in the prompt are actually well covered: gamma-flip divergence has extensive tests (src/lib/providers/gex-cross-validation-core.test.ts covers ±2-strike tolerance, bistable-flip stability, hysteresis, and a documented 80-point live migration bug that is now regression-tested), and WATCH-status handling has its own watch-track.test.ts plus a design where WATCH candidates are documented as having 'NO ledger role' (src/lib/zerodte/intel.ts:109) so they structurally cannot enter graded outcomes. The Whop webhook signature path is the one with a genuine, total test-coverage absence.
EVIDENCE: src/app/api/webhook/whop/route.ts:182-205 (unwrap()/signature check, catch->400); no `*.test.ts` for this route found via `find . -path '*/api/webhook/whop*'` (only route.ts, no test file) and `grep -rln 'webhook/whop|whop/route|WHOP_WEBHOOK_SECRET' --include=*.test.ts src` returning only unrelated route-registry/no-store-header tests, not signature tests. Comparison: src/app/api/webhooks/clerk/route.test.ts exists and asserts svix header handling.

**CQ-172** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: There is precision-bound testing for the CLOSED-FORM Black-Scholes gamma implementation used by the depth-ladder feature, but NOT for the primary GEX-heatmap dollar-gamma path that consumes Polygon's provider-supplied per-strike `greeks.gamma` values — and the latter is the one the question is really asking about ('polygon-options-gex test suite'). In src/lib/gex-depth.test.ts (line 44-47): `it('bsGamma IS the derivative of bsDelta — not merely a plausible curve', ...)` computes a numeric derivative of bsDelta and asserts `Math.abs(numeric - bsGamma(S,K,T,sig)) < 1e-6` — this DOES catch a 1e-6-scale error in the closed-form BS-gamma formula itself (src/lib/gex-depth.ts:205). But the GEX heatmap's actual per-strike dollar-gamma summation in src/lib/providers/polygon-options-gex.ts (`const gamma = Number(c.greeks?.gamma ?? 0)` at lines 3232/4252/4279, then `gamma * oi * sharesPerContract * spot * spot * 0.01` at line 4289 / `signedGamma` at line 3258) reads gamma DIRECTLY off the Polygon response — there is no closed-form recomputation to check it against inside the automated test suite. Searching src/lib/providers/polygon-options-gex.test.ts (978 lines) for an exact golden-value assertion tying a specific input gamma/OI/spot to a specific expected dollar-gamma output finds none — its tests cover page-guard resolution, spot fallback, cache TTL, and higher-level `computeGexEvents` (flip crossings, wall breaks, sign flips) via CONSTRUCTED scenario objects, never a raw per-strike gamma value check with a numeric tolerance. So: if ONE strike's raw provider gamma value were off by 1e-6 (a float/rounding bug, a units mismatch, or a bad parse), nothing in polygon-options-gex.test.ts would catch it — the events tests only check qualitative behavior (did a flip cross, not the exact dollar total at a given gamma). The only thing that WOULD catch a live provider-gamma discrepancy is the live audit script scripts/audit/gex-depth-validate.mjs (documented in CLAUDE.md) which cross-checks closed-form BS gamma vs the provider's own gamma at spot — but that is a manual, non-CI audit tool, not a committed regression test, and it is documented to tolerate a real ~0.1%-22% RAW disagreement depending on ticker (dividend yield effects), which is orders of magnitude looser than 1e-6 anyway.
EVIDENCE: src/lib/gex-depth.test.ts:44-47 `Math.abs(numeric - bsGamma(S,K,T,sig)) < 1e-6` (precision-bound, but only for the depth-ladder's own closed-form gamma, not the heatmap path). src/lib/providers/polygon-options-gex.ts:3232,4252,4279 read `c.greeks?.gamma` verbatim from the provider; line 4289 computes `gamma * oi * sharesPerContract * spot * spot * 0.01` with no reconciliation test. `grep -n 'dollar|0\.01|sharesPerContract' src/lib/providers/polygon-options-gex.test.ts` shows no exact-value golden assertions on this formula. CLAUDE.md's own audit-toolkit entry for gex-depth-validate.mjs documents raw BS-vs-provider gamma agreement as low as 78.3%-99.9% depending on ticker (SPY/QQQ/IWM dividend-yield gap), which is the honest live tolerance — nowhere near 1e-6 — and that script is a manual audit, not part of `npm test`.

**CQ-176** | PARTIALLY PROVEN

**CQ-182** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: The fix is real and a test exists in CI, but it is NOT the kind of test the question describes. `src/features/nighthawk/lib/record-honesty-contract.test.ts` (added in commit 7125d1a0d, #3434) adds a test named 'db.ts: pruneNighthawkPlayOutcomesForEdition can never delete a graded row — SQL guard, not caller discipline' that does a STATIC regex match against the literal SQL text in `src/lib/db.ts` (`assert.match(fn, /DELETE FROM nighthawk_play_outcomes\s+WHERE edition_for = \$1::date\s+AND NOT \(ticker = ANY\(\$2::varchar\[\]\)\)\s+AND outcome = 'pending'/)`). It never spins up a DB, never inserts a graded row, and never actually attempts a DELETE and observes it fail — it only proves the source string contains the guard clause. This is real regression protection against someone silently deleting the `AND outcome = 'pending'` clause, but it is a source-text assertion, not a functional/integration test against a live or in-memory Postgres. The fix itself (adding `AND outcome = 'pending'` to the DELETE in `pruneNighthawkPlayOutcomesForEdition`, src/lib/db.ts) is real and matches every sibling write's own guard pattern. The test file is a normal `*.test.ts` under `src/`, which `npm test` (scripts/run-tests.mjs, the exact command CI's `verify` gate runs) globs and executes, so it IS still in CI — just not the integration-style test the question assumes exists.
EVIDENCE: git show 7125d1a0d — diff adds the test at src/features/nighthawk/lib/record-honesty-contract.test.ts (new ~32 lines) and the SQL guard at src/lib/db.ts (+20 lines). Commit message itself: 'Regression test added to record-honesty-contract.test.ts (confirmed RED pre-fix, GREEN post-fix)' — describes a source-guard test, consistent with what was found. package.json:'test': 'node scripts/run-tests.mjs' runs the whole *.test.ts suite including this file.

So: empty vs broken are indeed indistinguishable in this component, and no copy tells the member when to check back.
EVIDENCE: src/features/vector/components/VectorScanner.tsx:55-67 (loading branch line 55-59; `if (error || !data?.rows?.length) return <p ...>Universe snapshot unavailable — pick a symbol above.</p>` at lines 61-67); lines 91-104 (the staleness/no-match branches that are only reached with data present). **CQ-193** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: Strong code-level affordances exist for both asks. Sorting: each sortable column header is a real `<button>` (native Tab-reachable) with `aria-sort` correctly toggled ascending/descending/none and a `toggleSort` handler wired to `onClick` (src/features/helix/components/HelixFlowTable.tsx:443-462), so a keyboard user can Tab to a header and press Enter/Space to sort — no mouse required. Opening the detail drawer: each data row is a `role="row"` div made focusable (`tabIndex={interactive ? 0 : undefined}`) with an `onKeyDown` handler that fires the same `onContractClick`/`onTickerClick` callback on Enter or Space (lines 526,553-564), so a focused row can open the drawer via keyboard. The opened drawer itself (`ContractDrilldownDrawer` → shared `Drawer`/`Modal` in src/components/ui/Modal.tsx:43-52,68-71,115-116) has a genuine accessible-dialog implementation: `role="dialog"`, `aria-modal="true"`, a manual focus trap via `useFocusTrap` (Tab/Shift+Tab wrap), Escape-to-close, and focus restored to the opener on close — this is a real, non-cosmetic a11y implementation, not a stub. One real gap: within each row, the ticker-symbol text and the star-toggle are ALSO independently focusable/interactive (`role={onTickerClick ? "button" : undefined}`, `tabIndex={onTickerClick ? 0 : undefined}` at lines 168-183, plus a separate `<button>` star at 155-166) nested inside a row that is itself a focusable/keydown-activatable target — this produces overlapping/nested tab stops whose exact Tab-order behavior (does Tab visit row→star→ticker-text, or does the outer row's own tabIndex get skipped once children are focusable?) cannot be settled from source alone and needs a live keyboard walkthrough.
EVIDENCE: src/features/helix/components/HelixFlowTable.tsx:443-462 (sort button + aria-sort), 526,553-564 (row tabIndex + onKeyDown Enter/Space), 155-186 (nested star button + ticker span both independently focusable inside the row); src/components/ui/Modal.tsx:43-52 (doc comment: 'Accessible dialog — role="dialog" aria-modal, scrim, Esc-to-close, a manual focus trap'), :68-71 (`useFocusTrap(panelRef, {active: open, onEscape: closeOnEsc ? onClose : undefined})`), :115-116 (`role="dialog" aria-modal="true"`), :182 (Drawer wraps Modal, same guarantees). Live check that would fully prove tab order: an actual keyboard-only walkthrough recording on `/flows` (HELIX desk) — Tab from the sort headers into a row, verify visited order for star→ticker→row, Enter to open the drawer, Tab through its contents, Esc to close and confirm focus returns to the originating row.

**CQ-194** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: `prefers-reduced-motion` is respected in two independent, verifiable ways. (1) Globally: `src/components/MotionProvider.tsx:22-36` wraps the whole app in framer-motion's `<MotionConfig reducedMotion="user">`, which the file's own doc comment states means 'every framer-motion animation respects OS prefers-reduced-motion' — this is the mechanism that would cover any chart-switch transition built with framer-motion. (2) Feature-specific: several chart/visual components do their own explicit `matchMedia("(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)")` checks and drop to a flat/static render — `src/features/meridian/components/meridian-spatial.tsx:55` ('prefers-reduced-motion and coarse pointers drop straight to the flat render'), `src/features/vector/lib/vector-wall-rail-primitive.ts:360`, and Tailwind's `motion-safe:` utility is used directly in Thermal's transition classes (`ThermalCompareStrip.tsx:112`, `GexHeatmap.tsx:938,1366` — `motion-safe:transition-all motion-safe:duration-300` on the heatmap bar-fill/wall-position transitions, which specifically gates the bar-fill animation an INP-sensitive user would hit on ticker switch). What is NOT provable from static code: whether an actual ticker-switch on a live chart (e.g. Vector's main price/GEX chart re-render) produces a measurable INP spike that blocks input — that requires a live Interaction-to-Next-Paint measurement, which no harness in this repo currently performs (repo-wide grep for INP/web-vitals instrumentation was empty).
EVIDENCE: src/components/MotionProvider.tsx:9-11,20,24 (MotionConfig reducedMotion="user", 180ms default transition); src/features/meridian/components/meridian-spatial.tsx:23,55 (explicit matchMedia reduced-motion check, drops to flat render); src/features/vector/lib/vector-wall-rail-primitive.ts:82,360 (same pattern); src/features/thermal/components/ThermalCompareStrip.tsx:112 and GexHeatmap.tsx:938,1366 (`motion-safe:transition-all motion-safe:duration-300` on live value transitions). Live check to prove/disprove an INP spike on ticker switch: Chrome DevTools Performance trace (or `web-vitals` INP observer injected via `proxy-browser.cjs`) while rapidly switching tickers on `/vector` or `/heatmap` in production, comparing INP under normal vs `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` emulation.

**CQ-205** | PARTIALLY PROVEN
A: There is no dedicated 'thank-you page' — conversion tracking instead relies on TIER-CHANGE DETECTION mounted globally in `AppShellProviders.tsx` (`<Ga4ConversionTracker/>`), which fires GA4 `purchase` + Google Ads conversion + X pixel the first time it observes the member's tier flip from non-paid to `community`-or-above on ANY subsequent authenticated page load, gated by a `localStorage` once-per-user flag (`bo_ga4_purchase_fired:<userId>`). This means attribution depends on the member's NEXT app page load after returning from Whop, not a guaranteed same-request fire on the Whop return URL itself — if a member closes the tab right after Whop checkout and never reopens the app in that browser (or in private mode where the localStorage flag can't persist, risking a re-fire on the next session), the conversion event does not fire, or could double-fire. Purchase value is recovered from a remembered clicked-plan (`checkout-plans.ts`) rather than a flat tier estimate, which is a genuine correctness safeguard against under/over-valuing yearly vs monthly, but doesn't change the timing-reliability concern above. Whether this actually produces trustworthy attributed revenue in Google Ads requires a live conversion-firing test (visit /pricing, click a plan, complete a real or sandbox Whop checkout, and watch GA4 DebugView / Google Ads conversion diagnostics) which was not run in this pass.
EVIDENCE: src/components/analytics/Ga4ConversionTracker.tsx:1-76 (full tier-transition-triggered fire logic, localStorage flag at line 36-38); src/components/providers/AppShellProviders.tsx:17,30 (global mount point, not page-scoped); src/lib/analytics/checkout-plans.ts (plan-value recovery across the Whop round-trip).
